# THEOPHRASTUS
# THE GERMAN,
# PARACELSUS,
# Of all Physicians and Philosophers
# in the universe,
# easily the Foremost.
*On the Restored True Practice of*
*Both Medicines.* <note>original: "vtriuſque Medicinæ." This likely refers to Paracelsus’s system of treating both the physical body and the "astral" or spiritual body.</note>

### BOOK THE FIRST.
Gerard Dorn, Doctor of Medicine and German interpreter, collecting [these works] into this order. <note>Gerard Dorn was a 16th-century philosopher who dedicated much of his life to translating Paracelsus’s difficult Swiss-German manuscripts into Latin to reach a wider European audience.</note>
To the most Illustrious and most Powerful Prince, Lord **FRANCIS**
**VALOIS**, Duke of Anjou, Berry,
Alençon, Touraine, etc. <note>original: "Franciscum Valesium." This refers to the youngest son of Catherine de' Medici and brother to the King of France.</note>

->LET NO MAN BELONG TO ANOTHER<-
->WHO CAN BELONG TO HIMSELF<- <note>original: "Alterius non sit qui suus esse potest." This was Paracelsus’s famous personal motto, emphasizing his independence from traditional academic authorities like Galen or Avicenna.</note>
->THE LIKENESS OF THEOPHRASTUS PARACELSUS AT THE AGE OF 47<-

AT LYON
FOR JACQUES DU P<unclear>UIS</unclear>
1578.
With the privilege of the Imperial and Royal Majesties for ten years. <note>A "privilege" was a legal protection, similar to a modern copyright, preventing others from printing this specific edition for a set period.</note>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This is the opening page of a dedicatory epistle. There is no preceding page for continuity.</meta>

[Decorative border of floral and scrollwork motifs]

### ->TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS<-
### ->AND MOST POWERFUL PRINCE,<-
### ->**FRANCIS OF VALOIS,**<-
### ->Royal Son of France, Duke<-
### ->of Alençon, etc.<-

\*

[Historiated initial T depicting two figures in a landscape] **T**HE great German, Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who credited himself with overturning ancient medical traditions.</note>—the most recent awakener of Medical Philosophy, which had long been lulled to sleep and buried along with the other arts—hands down to us the teaching <note>original: *Tradit nobis*</note> that all wisdom depends on the one and only Father, God the giver of light. He teaches that it flows down to us, as His children, partly by hereditary right and partly as a gift of His generosity. For that good Father of all bestows wisdom and the arts upon everyone in general and each person individually in equal measure, so that no experienced or industrious man is permitted to boast over even the least unlearned <term>idiotam: In this Renaissance context, the term "idiot" refers to a "layperson" or someone without formal academic training, rather than a person with a cognitive disability.</term> or idle man, nor should he presume anything of himself. Just as He gave no person more or fewer limbs in their body than others, but gave to this one the same as that one, so too He willed that the minds of all people should be made equally capable and teachable in His wisdom. It is therefore unjust for one man to learn from another as if either were more fortunate than the other in this regard. In every person, the arts lie hidden as if they were sleeping; and while a faculty may be awake in one, it is granted from heaven to awaken that sleeping faculty in another—not, however, to introduce it from the outside. For all things are present within everyone from the very beginning. Indeed, if there is deliberation regarding a doubtful matter—

<summary>This page opens a dedication to Francis of Valois, Duke of Alençon, using the philosophy of Paracelsus to argue that all human knowledge is a divine gift inherent in everyone from birth.</summary>
<keywords>Francis of Valois, Paracelsus, Medical Philosophy, divine wisdom, innate knowledge, Duke of Alençon, egalitarianism in learning</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page is part of an introductory epistle (letter) found in a 16th or 17th-century medical or philosophical treatise. It follows a discussion on how knowledge is shared and recognized among people, leading into a defense of the reformer Paracelsus.</meta>

->E P I S T L E<-

...when many people gather to deliberate, and a matter is unknown to all except for one person, if that one person unties the knot and the rest of the group agrees with him, it is clear to all others that the same reason and knowledge existed within themselves. While this insight was "sleeping" within them, they approve of it once it has been awakened by the one in whom it was already "awake." Otherwise, they could by no means have acquiesced to the opinion of a single man, nor could they admit that he had spoken of things that had not previously entered their minds. What reason, then, does that man have to boast that his judgment was active at that particular moment? For in others, something else—and perhaps something better and more excellent—could be awakened in its own time, which might happen to be dormant in that very man. 

Conversely, no one can rightly complain that less has been bestowed upon them than upon another. For a person who is slumbering—and thus, like a sleeper, forgetting his duties—has the power to rouse himself through vigilance and diligence toward the aptitude and industry hidden within him. Or, he might patiently accept the promptings of those who wake him, using his talent <term>talent: a reference to the biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14-30), where individuals are expected to invest the gifts or resources given to them by God rather than hiding them</term> for interest and profit, rather than burying it and uselessly scorning it. 

Moved by pity for this calamity and stirred by a zeal for charity, Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who challenged traditional medical authorities like Galen and Avicenna</note> strives daily with all his might—and though he has passed away, he continues through his writings—to awaken those who are sleeping in the medical and philosophical professions and to recall them, as if from darkness to light. Since he saw that they were taught by men rather than from the Light of Nature <term>Light of Nature: *Lumen Naturae*, a concept in Paracelsian philosophy suggesting that the natural world contains divine wisdom that humans can perceive through observation and experience</term> (which is a divine light exercising its brilliance everywhere within this great world), he places this "mirror" before their eyes. He does this so that they might be able to see and learn from it—just as one does from a book—and distinguish between good and evil, as well as truth and falsehood, more exactly and perfectly than they have been able to do from the various writings of their teachers up until now. Therefore, he insists everywhere...

<summary>This page argues that all humans possess latent knowledge that can be "awakened" by a teacher, using this as a justification for the work of Paracelsus. It presents Paracelsus as a guide who directs students away from outdated textbooks and toward the "Light of Nature" found in the world itself.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Light of Nature, medical reform, innate knowledge, Parable of the Talents, 16th-century philosophy, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page appears to be part of a dedication or introductory preface, likely for a work of natural philosophy or medicine (possibly Paracelsian, given the terminology). It establishes the relationship between the "great world" (macrocosm) and the "little world" of man (microcosm).</meta>

->**TO THE DEDICATION.**<-

From the anatomy of the great world <note>original: "magni mundi" — This refers to the Macrocosm, the universe as a whole.</note>, worldly wisdom must be sought, as if from the light of nature <term>light of nature: a Renaissance concept (Lumen Naturae) referring to the innate knowledge or divine wisdom visible in the physical world</term>; no differently than from the darkness of the same, every kind of vice is to be known and avoided, and that through quite varied examples. 

Man (he says) is not only a son of God, but also of all His creatures, which nature encompasses. Since the final creature <note>Man was traditionally considered the "last" or "pinnacle" of creation in the Genesis narrative.</note>, taken from the mud, consists also of those things which were created before him and for his sake—namely, of the nature of the beasts and of the firmament. For it is plain to see, not only that the beasts live in the manner of men, but what is more, that the lives of the latter are transmuted into the nature of the former. 

But truly, because a son cannot be well known from himself, nor better than from his father, the properties of the animal nature, the firmament, and the elements are necessary to be known within man. Birds build nests; men build houses and palaces. That these should come together and be joined, male and female, to dwell and live as one, animal reason itself persuades; for we see the beasts do no differently. 

Bees, herrings <note>original: "haleca" — Herrings were frequently used as examples of social order and migration in early natural histories.</note>, and cranes grant the first place to their leaders and kings, and offer obedience: in this very thing, they even surpass us humans. And the ants convict us of laziness; by way of encouragement, they teach us to collect in summer what we need in the winter time, and for the young to prepare the necessities of life for old age. Why, I ask, are we less moved by these things, which reflect some specimen of virtue, than by those things which fight diametrically against virtue? Is it not because that most depraved nature which dwells in us attempts to extinguish the light with its shadows, twisting our animal side toward its own vices. Just as when we see dogs fighting and tearing each other apart for a female dog in heat: what else do men do for a common woman? To this, through wars, a comparison...

* 3 comparison <note>This is a catchword at the bottom of the page, indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>This page argues that human behavior and wisdom must be understood by studying the "great world" and the animal kingdom, noting that humans often inherit both the virtues and the base vices of animals.</summary>
<keywords>Macrocosm, Light of Nature, Microcosm, animal behavior, social order, natural philosophy, virtue and vice</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical and astrological discussion regarding the relationship between human nature, animal instincts, and the influence of the celestial bodies (the firmament). It follows a common Renaissance theme of "Man as Microcosm," where human behavior is mirrored in the animal kingdom and governed by planetary shifts.</meta>

->E P I S T L E<-

...the nature of sexual immorality <note>original: "ratio fornicationis"</note> is compared to the work of dogs. Dogs likewise excel in the envy of wanting to possess everything and leaving nothing for others; yet men with these kinds of vices resemble dogs, or rather, they far surpass them. They resemble not only dogs, but also foxes in their cunning, lions in their cruelty and ferocity, and other such creatures.

Again, man is a "son of the firmament" <note>The "firmament" refers to the sphere of the stars; the author suggests man's physical, animal body is subject to astrological influence.</note> because of his brute nature; for the brute animal is subject to the firmament. For example, the Lamb <note>original: "Agnus." Likely a reference to the zodiac sign Aries, where the Sun is exalted, representing a mild and solar temperament.</note> is the Sun, as is the man who is gentle in his character and life. Saturn is the Wolf, and the man who lives by plunder and robbery is the Bear. The Rooster is the son of Mars, as is the man driven by war and anger.

Therefore, the firmament rules only the brute animal nature within man through its natural inclination, and nothing more. Man, on the contrary, is able to resist the stars and the firmament through wisdom, but he cannot do so through his brute nature alone. We do not discuss this wisdom here, as it belongs to another and more sublime place. Our discourse concerns only worldly wisdom, which, when compared to the other <insert>divine wisdom</insert>, is mere foolishness and does not appear before the face of God, since it dies along with the man's own brute nature.

Why, then, do the students of this worldly wisdom believe they please God more than others who understand less? Why do they stubbornly despise them, even though those others are far happier? If we believe that knowing nothing makes life happy—because he who received nothing is held to no account for his "talent" <note>A reference to the Biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), where servants are judged based on how they invested what they were given.</note>—then it will be much more blessed for a smaller burden of responsibility to rest upon a person through wisdom.

These things are said not to discourage men from the study of wisdom, but rather so that, having recognized the distinction between the animalistic and the rational, they might move closer to that path which joins integrity with necessity (the reason why the arts were invented). This is better than following the path that only indulges its own appetites for the sake of utility, just as irrational brutes do. Likewise, it is so that those who have been granted a greater share of reason might bear with others humanely and humbly, who are lower...

<summary>The author explores how human vices mirror animal traits and how the "brute" side of humanity is influenced by astrology. He argues that while stars may incline the animal body, true wisdom allows a person to resist these impulses and live with moral integrity.</summary>

<keywords>astrology, firmament, animal symbolism, Saturn, Mars, wisdom vs. instinct, Parable of the Talents, ethics, human nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a formal dedication, likely from a 16th or 17th-century philosophical or medical treatise. The author is arguing that true knowledge comes from observing nature (the "Light of Nature") rather than just academic study.</meta>

# DEDICATION

...having attained the matter more humbly, because they are human beings, so that they may also consider themselves worthy of honor. Who, I ask, taught the physician the powers of the <term>Snakeroot herb<note>original: "herbæ serpentariæ"; a plant traditionally used to treat snakebites and other ailments. The author suggests it was discovered by watching snakes heal themselves.</note></term>? Was it not the <term>Light of Nature<note>original: "lux naturæ"; a key Renaissance concept meaning the innate wisdom of the natural world, which reflects the mind of God and can be understood through observation.</note></term> acting through the serpent? 

And perhaps the first man to notice this—observing a snake curing its own injury—was a common peasant. It is no wonder, then, that the destiny of such discoveries belongs more to the folk of the woods. In this way, it is found that the physician and the wise man have been taught by the peasant, rather than the peasant by the physician. Therefore, nothing should be rejected that comes forth into the light of experience from the Light of Nature, whether it is revealed through a nobleman, a city-dweller, or a countryman through diligent observation. 

We see that everyone can be moved by the animals—no less toward virtues than toward vices. It is not wrong, but worthy of a wise man, to reject these vices and choose those virtues as a model upon which to build one's life and character. Since all virtue comes from God alone (even in the animals), we may learn: 

*   From the **dog**, loyalty (casting aside flattery and envy); 
*   From the **serpent**, prudence (leaving behind treachery); 
*   From the **donkey**, patience (neglecting laziness and sluggishness); 
*   From the **dove**, simplicity. 

Nor are there lacking among other creatures those who observe governance and fairness, as can be gathered from their own natural histories. 

Whoever, therefore, has been taught from the "Book of the Light of Nature" must be considered the legitimate children of wisdom, because they receive their arts from God. Indeed, all things are full of divine light and wisdom—at least for those whose "eye" is the mind. 

On the contrary, those who rely only on their own imaginations and doubtful opinions, or are instructed only in the doctrines of men, sow a <term>bastard seed<note>original: "spurium germen"; referring to false or illegitimate knowledge that lacks a foundation in natural law or divine truth.</note></term> among all nations. They perform and hand down every task in a monstrous and backward way; then they take great care to mask their "illegitimate" works so that, being decorated on the outside, they might be thought legitimate. From there, in place of

->* 4          health<-

<summary>The author argues that true wisdom and medical knowledge are derived from the 'Light of Nature' and the observation of animals and common folk, rather than academic doctrines. He lists specific virtues that humans should emulate from animals, such as the loyalty of dogs and the patience of donkeys.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page is the opening of a dedicatory epistle (a formal introductory letter) for a Latin edition of works by Paracelsus. The translator explains why Paracelsus’s revolutionary approach to medicine is necessary and acknowledges his patrons.</meta>

-># E P I S T L E<-

In the study of health within **Medicine**, and in the records regarding the introduction of death, various teachings arise. It is no different in other arts, such as **Philosophy**, where the overthrows of kingdoms, hatreds, disagreements, and similar monstrous and wicked disasters of every kind are born. To turn away such great errors, especially in Medicine, **Paracelsus** laid the true foundations with tireless labor and the highest devotion. He placed these foundations—as is only right—in the <term>light of nature</term> <note>original: *luce naturae*; a key concept in Paracelsian thought referring to knowledge gained through the direct observation of the natural world and divine revelation rather than through ancient textbooks</note>. However, these truths must be sought **through fire** <note>original: *per ignem*; this refers to the alchemical or "spagyric" process of using heat to separate and purify the medicinal essences of plants and minerals</note>, so that the hidden essence may come to light, wherein lies the medicinal power: a perfect and precious pearl.

For just as the brute animals do, the elements stir man in the <term>lesser globe</term> <note>original: *minore globo*; the Microcosm, or the human body, which was believed to contain all the elements of the universe</note> no less than the stars through the firmament stir the <term>greater globe</term> <note>original: *maiore [globo]*; the Macrocosm, or the universe as a whole</note>. Anyone who possesses judgment and talent is compelled to admit, based on what has been stated, that no other or better foundations can be found than those he sets before our eyes. By these, he strives to rouse us and ensure that we return to the true goal of medicine, from which men have wandered far and wide until now.

Therefore, **Most Illustrious Prince**, several years ago, I translated many books by this same most influential author (originally written in the **German language**) into **Latin**. I did this for the sake of those nations that do not speak German, as I saw that the **French**, above all others, delighted in that kind of philosophy and healing. Many distinguished and excellent men encouraged me not to cease from the undertaking I had begun. Among these, especially—as one well-versed in the noble arts—**Petrus Degrantrye**, the Ordinary Master of the Household to your Most Illustrious Highness, did not hesitate to greatly promote these efforts of mine. He continued this support until your Most Illustrious Highness deigned to appoint me to the regular duty of the **physical translation** <note>referring here to *physica*, the study of natural science and medicine</note> and the interpretation of those same books, even though I am unworthy...
                                                                           
<margin>reason</margin> <note>The Latin text ends on a partial word "ratio," likely beginning a phrase like "by reason of" or "the reasoning" on the following page.</note>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle of a translated work, likely from the late 16th or early 17th century, where the translator addresses their patron regarding the translation of German alchemical or medical texts into Latin and French.</meta>

# DEDICATION

Reason therefore demands that whatever strength remains in me, I shall diligently devote all of it to this matter—even to the point of exhaustion if necessary—and do what befits a faithful servant and translator. Let this present little work be my witness from the beginning; although small, it is not to be utterly despised because of the utility it provides to readers. For it will grow in its own time (God willing) so that it may even find a place among other works. I frankly confess that I owe much more excellent things to the splendor of Your Most Illustrious Highness <note>Original Latin: "Illuſtriſſimo C.T." abbreviation for "Celsitudo Tua," a formal address meaning "Your Highness."</note>. Once they have emerged into the light from the caves <note>A metaphor for being hidden, unpublished, or obscure.</note> in which they have been held until now, and as the various German writings of this author come into my hands, I will endeavor—with the favor of the heavens—to bring them into your library, rendered into Latin or French with the utmost faithfulness and diligence to which I am bound to Your Most Illustrious Highness. 

In this way, all brilliant minds who are students of the <term>secrets of nature</term> <note>This refers to "arcana," the hidden operations of alchemy, chemistry, and natural philosophy.</note> may give thanks and praise to the name of Your Most Illustrious Highness. Through your benefit and at your expense, it will now be a pleasure to satisfy that thirst—which has been contracted in France for a long tract of time—with much more fruitful philosophical waves and medical springs than were available before. 

What is more worthy of such a great Prince, or more glorious in this life, than to establish and promote the study of virtues? And how much more so to wish to awaken and restore the arts—which have been put to sleep by the injustice of time and corrupted by the envy of men—and to restore them, as if from the dead, into living Ideas? In this matter, the ancestors of Your Most Illustrious Highness, the Kings of France, have always been famous and earned perpetual praise for themselves. It is clearer than daylight that Your Most Illustrious Highness follows in their footsteps, as you take care with the greatest diligence that these books are brought into France for the common use and benefit of all.

First, therefore, [I offer] this book of Practice...

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This is the opening preface of Gerard Dorn’s edition of Paracelsus’s "Practica." Dorn was a 16th-century alchemist and a key figure in translating Paracelsus's German works into Latin to make them accessible to the international scholarly community.</meta>

->GERARD DORN’S PREFACE TO THE READER.<-

You will perhaps wonder, friend reader, that the *Practice* <note>original: *Practicam*; likely referring to Paracelsus's practical manuals on medicine or chemistry</note> of Paracelsus, as if an almost immense mass, has been reduced into an atom. If, however, you consider the goal of this study of ours, I hope you will not take it poorly, nor will you disapprove of these labors of mine, which I have freely undertaken for your sake. For I saw in these past days, when I was in France, that many people complained about the confused sequence of this writing—and not without merit. But it was hidden from them that scarcely a thousandth part of that author’s writings has come to light until now, and even that part is mutilated and torn from its limbs, so that one could hardly collect the fragments or crumbs falling from the table. <margin>Dorn notes here that only a tiny, fragmented portion of Paracelsus's massive body of work had been published by the late 1500s.</margin> We enjoy these fragments for the time being until greater things come to pass. 

The Almighty Lord has procured for us no small favor in this matter from the most Illustrious Prince Francis of Valois, son of the King, now in France <note>Francis, Duke of Anjou and Alençon (1555–1584), the youngest son of King Henry II of France and a patron of the arts and sciences</note>. By his virtues and authority, it will be possible at some time in France to see far greater and more excellent monuments <note>Dorn uses "monuments" to mean significant intellectual or written works</note> of Paracelsus than these which I brought there—though I did so not without the rebuke of some of our fellow Germans. 

These men said that I ought not to plunder the secrets of a German man outside his native soil and distribute them to other nations through translations. When they saw that I did not desist from my undertaking, but instead proceeded all the more eagerly, they themselves—I know not by what impulse—began to translate the books of that man. I see nothing that could have happened more pleasantly to me than that my efforts should be carried even further; for they are aimed especially at this: that not only in Germany, but even through the whole world (if it were possible), the sick may be helped in a brotherly way. But let me return to from whence I digressed...

<summary>Gerard Dorn introduces his condensed version of Paracelsus's medical teachings, defending his decision to translate these "German secrets" into Latin for the benefit of the international medical community and the sick.</summary>
<keywords>Gerard Dorn, Paracelsus, translation, France, Francis of Valois, medical ethics, German intellectual property, alchemy, 16th-century medicine</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
It was my intention to gather into one volume the <term>Practice of Medicine</term> <note>original: "Practica"; refers to the practical application of medical treatments, clinical cases, and recipes.</note>, which is otherwise scattered across various small books. By doing so, the reader staying at home may quickly find—as if from an index—where to consult the author’s thoughts on specific diseases within the <term>Theory of Medicine</term> <note>original: "Theorica"; the study of the underlying causes, philosophical principles, and the nature of health and disease.</note>. Likewise, when traveling abroad, this single volume will serve you as well as if you were carrying all the other books with you.

For this reason, I have left a sufficiently wide margin for each recipe, so that you may write down with your pen any annotations drawn from the *Theory* at your own discretion. Following this same order, I have planned (God willing) to complete the remaining books of *Practice* and *Theory*. 

Nor did I think it enough to provide only this; I also intended that wherever something obscure occurs, it should be revealed through some elucidation and brief interpretations, as far as they can be understood. As soon as I finish the *Practice* (of which you now hold the first book), you will see me proceed to the *Theory* itself. I will follow the order established by our author, especially in medical matters, where he maintains with invincible arguments that the former must be sought from the latter, as if from the <term>light of nature</term> <note>original: "luce naturae"; a philosophical concept, often associated with Paracelsus, suggesting that true medical and spiritual knowledge is gained through the direct observation of the natural world.</note>.

Meanwhile, kind reader, use these works as your own and enjoy them while we prepare more. If I perceive that these are pleasing to you, I will not delay long in producing more, provided we have the resources for them. 

Farewell, and consider me as devoted to you as I know you are to this profession.

[*]

<summary>In this preface, the editor explains his project of consolidating scattered medical writings into a portable volume with wide margins for reader notes. He emphasizes the importance of connecting practical treatments (Practice) with their underlying philosophical principles (Theory).</summary>
<keywords>medical education, Practice vs Theory, medical recipes, light of nature, medical editing, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

...must be punished. We therefore command each and every one of our subjects, and those of the Holy Empire and of our hereditary kingdoms and dominions, both faithful and beloved, whether they be members of the Clergy or the Laity, of whatever state, grade, order, condition, or dignity they may be, but especially those who are established in magistracies and who exercise the administration of law and justice—whether in the name of their superiors or in their own name and place—that they do not allow anyone to violate, despise, or neglect this privilege or <term>interdict</term><note>A formal prohibition or decree.</note> of ours with impunity. Rather, if they find any who are defiant, they shall see to it that they are struck with the prescribed penalty and coerced by whatever right means possible, insofar as they themselves wish to avoid our gravest indignation and the same fine. In witness of these letters, signed with our own hand and fortified with the impression of our Imperial seal. Given in our City of Vienna, on the 7th day of the month of May, in the Year of our Lord 1578. In the third year of our Roman reign, the sixth of our Hungarian, and the third of our Bohemian.

->ON THE RESTORED
TRUE PRACTICE OF
BOTH MEDICINES
BOOK ONE.<-
<note>original: "Vtriusque Medicinae" - This refers to the reconciliation of traditional Greek-Arabic medicine with the emerging chemical medicine of the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>

->Containing the method for curing diseases
in alphabetical order.<-

*Cure for Corrosive Ulcers.*
<term>Esthiomenos: From the Greek for "eating," these were gnawing, spreading ulcers or gangrenous sores that consume the flesh.</term>

Take 3 ounces of Orpiment. <term>Auripigmentum: A yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used in medicine for its caustic properties to "eat away" diseased flesh.</term>
Take 6 ounces of burnt alum.
Reduce these and let an alkali be made.

*Additional ingredients:*
To 6 ounces of this preparation:
Add 2 ounces of liquid of mummy. <term>Mumia: A medicinal substance often derived from bitumen or, in some historical periods, processed from the remains of embalmed bodies, used to stop bleeding and heal wounds.</term>
Add 1 and a half ounces of oil of roses.
Add moss of darkness <note>original: "Musci caliginis," likely referring to a specific type of soot or a fine mineral deposit</note> and psyllium seeds, in an amount equal to the weight of all the other ingredients. Let a poultice or ointment be made from these.
If, however, burning sensations or inflammations are present, the ulcers are to be anointed with oil of camphor before this ointment or poultice is applied.
From the *Book of Preparations in Arsenic*.

->*The treatment of poisonous burns caused by metals, on account of their internal mercury.*<-

*The extinguishing of the heat must first proceed in this manner:*

<term>Take<note>The symbol ♃ (the sign of Jupiter) was commonly used in medical manuscripts as an abbreviation for the Latin "Recipe," meaning "Take."</note></term> pork lard, which, having been melted and made very hot, should be cast into the water of <term>nightshade<note>original: "solani vel solatri"; nightshade was frequently used in early medicine for its cooling and sedative properties when applied topically.</note></term>, and stirred in it with a small stick until it is reduced to the form of an ointment; let it be placed over [the burn] until the heat is completely extinguished. Afterwards, let them be treated with the common <term>miners’<note>original: "fodicatorum"; likely referring to specialized medical preparations used by or for those working in mines, who were frequently exposed to metallic toxins and heavy-metal burns.</note></term> stictic plasters mentioned above.

->*Extinguishing a Burn caused by Mercury.*<-

Place a linen cloth moistened with warm milk over the area twenty times, or even more, until the fire is extinguished. Afterward, let it be treated with ointments made of butter or pig fat applied over it until the heat is fully gone; once this is achieved, it shall be cured with the miners’ stictic ointments made with <term>colophony<note>original: "colophonia"; a translucent resin obtained from pine trees, used to give plasters their stickiness and protective qualities.</note></term>.

->### BOOK ONE. 3<-

Burns caused by Mercury <note>original: "Mercuriū"</note> most often occur during <term>amalgamations<note>The process of combining mercury with another metal to form an alloy.</note></term> and <term>sublimations of cinnabar<note>Cinnabar is a toxic mercury ore; sublimation is the process of heating it to vaporize and then purify the substance.</note></term>, from which workers have sometimes contracted toothaches or a trembling of the limbs. The teeth should be washed with lavender water. The limbs, however, should be washed with <term>aqua vitae<note>Literally "water of life," a term for concentrated distilled spirits or alcohol.</note></term>. Afterwards, one must use <term>healing plasters<note>The original uses "sticticis emplastris," referring to plasters intended to close wounds and heal scars.</note></term> as in other types of burns.

*The cure for burns resulting from salt, vitriol, and alum.*

Recipe:
Nut oil, half a pound <note>original: "lb. ß."</note>
Deer tallow, 1 pound <note>original: "lb. j."</note>
Butter quenched in water, 1 and a half pounds <note>original: "lb. j. ß."</note>
From these ingredients, thoroughly mixed together, let an ointment be made with which the burns should be treated twice every day until the heat is extinguished. Afterward, let them be further treated with wound ointments, balms, or powders, so that smaller scars are left behind by the treatment. Such burns are very frequent among dyers because of the <term>alums<note>Mineral salts used in the dyeing process to fix colors to fabric.</note></term> they use.

-># BOOK ONE.<-

Juice of nightshade <term>Solanum: A plant family often used in historical medicine for its cooling and sedative properties.</term>.
Water lily.
Crabs, and so on, in equal parts <note>The original Latin 'ana' is a medical shorthand meaning 'of each an equal amount.'</note>.

From the juice of these mixed together and pressed through a cloth, the area is to be washed and moistened to extinguish the heat. It should then be cared for with wound ointments and plasters for deep, piercing wounds.

Many such "extinguishers" can be made from the juices of snails, earthworms, frogs, river crabs, frogspawn, stonecrop, nightshade, water lily, and from metal dross <note>original: 'causura', likely referring to the scale or flakes that fall from metal during forging</note>, as well as vitriol <term>Atramento sutorio: Literally 'shoemaker’s ink,' this was typically green vitriol (ferrous sulfate) used as an astringent.</term>. But the preparation made from rose vinegar and the juice of river crabs is the most excellent of all.

However, if the burn seems to be fatal, it must be cooled from the inside, especially by drinking water or the juice of small damson plums <note>original: 'prunorum cypriorum minorum', referring to small plums from Cyprus.</note>, or the juice of the parsnip plant, milk, small beer <term>Zytho: A fermented grain drink, often used as a hydrating medicinal base.</term>, apple juice, and so on.

*The Great Surgery, Part 1, Book 3, Chapters 6, 7, 8, 9, 10.*

->*Red Powder of Antimony*<-

<margin>First, let a Salt be made from tartar <term>tartar: a salt-like deposit from wine barrels, used here to create potassium carbonate</term> calcined <term>calcined: heated to high temperatures to reduce a substance to a white powder</term> until white, then dissolved in warm distilled water. After shaking, let it stand still, and let the water be separated by pouring it into another vessel. Add more fresh water, similar to the first, and separate it as many times as needed until no more sharpness is perceived in the tartar, but rather it leaves the earth sweet. Once all the collected waters are driven off into the air by distillation, the salt of tartar will remain at the bottom.</margin>

Take 3 ounces of this salt and dissolve it in half a pound of water. Add half an ounce of ground antimony and let them boil together for a short time. The water becomes very clear, but as it cools, it turns red and congeals. Soon after, let a little distilled vinegar be poured in; the sharpness of the salt and the "malice" <note>original: *malitiáque*; refers to the toxic or harsh mineral impurities of the antimony</note> of the antimony will fall to the bottom. Let this liquid be poured off, and let sweet distilled water be poured on, repeated often until no sharpness is perceived by taste. Finally, let it be dried into a very deep red powder. The dose is one grain or half a grain.

->*A form of the Quintessence of Antimony*<-

Take 1 ½ ounces of the white flowers of antimony <term>flowers of antimony: the white oxide powder produced by heating antimony until it vaporizes and condenses</term>.
Melissa water <note>Lemon balm water, used for its calming properties.</note>
Circulated water of life <note>Distilled alcohol or spirits that have been refined through a cycle of evaporation and condensation.</note>
Celandine juice, 3 ounces of each.
Let them be digested... <note>Digestion in alchemy refers to the application of gentle, consistent heat over a long period.</note>

# 12 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES

## *The treatment of common abscesses using Oppodeltoch.*
<note>original: "Oppodeltoch" refers to Opodeldoc, a famous medical plaster or liniment popularized by Paracelsus.</note>

Take: Cooked litharge <term>litharge: lead monoxide, commonly used in historical ointments for its drying and healing properties</term> 3 ounces.
Of the four incarnatives <term>incarnatives: medicines or ingredients believed to promote the growth of new flesh in wounds</term> half an ounce.
Of liquefied gums 2 ounces.
Make a plaster according to the art <note>original: "secundum artem", a standard instruction for professional pharmaceutical preparation</note>. It would be best to lance the veins from which the abscesses arise, to remove the redness and corrupt blood; not only in these cases, but in all others.
From the *Book on Abscesses*, chapter 1.

## *Formula for water of rock salt.*

Take: Rock salt <term>sal gemmae: a pure, transparent form of common salt</term> half a pound.
Rainwater 1 pound.
Let it be distilled through a retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation</note> until the entire substance of the salt has risen through the glass.

## *The purification of gold.*

Take: Gold half an ounce.
Antimony 3 ounces.
Melt them in a crucible, etc.
From the *Book of Preparations regarding Gold*.

## *The secret of the prime matter.*

Take the seminal "prime matter" <term>prima materia: the fundamental, formless base of all matter in alchemy</term>; allow this to digest in a flask through resolutive digestion <note>a slow heating process intended to break down or dissolve a substance into its components</note> for one month. To this, let an addition of the "Monarchy" <note>original: "Monarchiæ"; in Paracelsian alchemy, this refers to a sovereign essence or governing principle</note> of equal weight be joined afterward, and allow it to be digested again, from one month into the next. Afterward, distill this material, and that which has ascended will be the secret <term>arcanum: a powerful medicinal secret or "inner essence" of a substance</term> of the prime matter.

*The secret*

**The secret of the philosophers' stone.**
Take <note>The symbol ♃, which traditionally represents the planet Jupiter or the metal tin, is used here as a medical shorthand for "Recipe" or "Take."</note> the element of mercury, and separate the pure from the impure. Afterward, let it be reverberated <note>Reverberation is a process of intense heating in a furnace where the flame is reflected back onto the material.</note> until it reaches a state of whiteness, and then let it be sublimated by means of sal ammoniac <note>original: "sal armoniacum," ammonium chloride, used to help substances vaporize and purify</note>, for as long as it takes to be dissolved. Let this be calcined <note>reduced to a powder through heat</note> and dissolved again, and then digested in a pelican <note>A specialized alchemical circulatory vessel with two "arms" that return condensed vapors to the base.</note> for one month; finally, let it be coagulated into a solid body. This substance is no longer used up, nor is it consumed in any way, but remains in the same state; the bodies which it penetrates become permanent even in the ash-test <note>original: "cineritio," referring to cupellation, a process where metals are tested for purity in a bed of bone ash</note>, and they cannot be reduced to nothing, nor diminished, nor altered. This stone removes the lesser essence and the superfluous quality from both sensible and insensible things, as has been narrated.

**The secret of the mercury of life.**
Take the essentialized mercury, which you have separated from all its superfluity, just as you separate the pure from the impure. Afterward, sublimate it with antimony so that both may ascend together and become one. Then let them be dissolved upon a marble slab, and coagulated up to the fourth time. When this is finished, you will have the mercury of life, by which all things are renewed and restored.

**The secret of the tincture.**
Take the essence of the members <note>original: "membrorum," likely referring to the constituent parts or "limbs" of the chemical substance</note>, from which you will separate the elements. Afterward, you will leave their fire placed in digestion for so long until nothing more remains at the bottom, nor does any matter appear there in a substantial form. Next, take the matter and the glass vessel, as it is sealed <note>original: "lutatum," meaning sealed airtight with "lute" or jeweler’s clay</note>

From *The Small Surgery*, Book 2, Chapter 7. <note>original: "Chirurgiæ Paruæ lib.ij.cap.vij." This refers to a work by Paracelsus, a foundational figure in toxicology and medical chemistry.</note>

*Oil of Orpiment, or Balsam of Arsenic.*
<note>Orpiment is a deep orange-yellow arsenic sulfide mineral. While highly toxic, it was a staple in alchemical medicine.</note>

Take: Crushed orpiment; to this add double its weight of saltpeter. Because of the harmful vapors, this must be done in the open air. Let the crucible glow with heat, and cast in the aforementioned material gradually until the entire substance of the saltpeter has ceased its bubbling and venting. Once this is observed, increase the fire for three or four hours, until the orpiment is seen melted at the bottom like butter. When the material has cooled, it will be bright and shining. Grind it thoroughly upon a marble slab and let it dissolve into a liquid in a damp place. This can then be distilled through a gourd-shaped flask <note>original: "cucurbitam"</note>. It is most effective for any ulcers, no matter how rotten or weeping they may be.

*Potable Gold.*
<note>Potable or "drinkable" gold was a legendary alchemical remedy thought to cure all ailments and even grant longevity.</note>

Once this [gold] has been purified by antimony, let it be dissolved into a powder in water or in a "green liquor of salt." This powder should be washed repeatedly with distilled "heavenly water" <note>original: "cœleſti aqua"; likely referring to highly rectified spirits or pure rainwater</note> until all saltiness departs. Alternatively, thin plates of gold may be calcined <term>calcined: reduced to powder through intense heat</term> by placing them between plates of lead in a furnace for 24 hours, then washed. Add enough spirit of wine to the powder so that it stands four fingers deep above it, and let them digest in a closed vessel...

20 ->ON THE CURING OF DISEASES<-

The statement situated [previously], requires some interpretation where it says: (*gold leaf, powdered, and dissolved into a juice*). By "powdered," I understand <term>calcined<note>Calcination is a process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical structure, often to make it brittle or more reactive.</note></term>, which is usually done in various ways.

First, when it is mixed with <term>quicksilver<note>The historical name for mercury.</note></term> that has been heated over a moderate fire; they call this <term>amalgamation<note>The process of combining mercury with another metal.</note></term>, <margin>How calcined gold is made</margin> a process not unknown to goldsmiths. Afterward, the whole of the quicksilver is driven into the air by fire, with the reddish powder of the gold remaining at the bottom of the vessel. <margin>1</margin>

There is also another method of grinding gold: if a thin sheet of it is placed over the opening of a suitable vessel, and beneath it in the vessel, either sublimated mercury or molten lead is held over hot coals so that it receives the vapor. <margin>2</margin> By this, the gold is rendered so brittle that it can be crushed with the fingers and reduced to a powder.

It can also be reduced to a fine dust in another way: <margin>3</margin> if it is mixed in a cauldron by the violence of fire during melting with six or more parts of its own weight in silver. Afterward, having been fashioned into thin leaves with hammers, both are dissolved in <term>aqua fortis<note>Literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid.</note></term>. All the silver passes into the liquid, while the gold settles at the bottom in the form of a blackish powder. This powder, like the others, must be <term>reverberated<note>Heated in a furnace where the flames are reflected or "reverberated" onto the material.</note></term> with <term>sal gemmae<note>Natural rock salt.</note></term> or common salt using a stronger fire, so that it loses its metallic form (if that is possible), about which more will be said below. 

Afterward, it must be washed with common water, or as in the preceding steps, with distilled vinegar, repeatedly and until it entirely sheds the sharpness of the <term>realgar-like<note>Realgar is a toxic arsenic sulfide mineral; the author is referring to the harsh, corrosive vapors of the acids and minerals used.</note></term> vapors, the *aqua fortis*, and the salts. This can easily be detected by applying it to the taste of the tongue. Once this is finished, it is ready for <term>digestion<note>In alchemy and early chemistry, "digestion" refers to the slow heating of a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or transform it.</note></term> with <term>aqua vitae<note>Literally "water of life," referring here to highly concentrated alcohol or spirit of wine.</note></term>.

It remains...

BOOK ONE. 21

It remains that, in the formula for <term>water of life</term> <note>original: "aquæ vitæ," a concentrated medicinal alcohol or distilled spirit used as a solvent</note>, we should declare what is signified by *Anthos* and *Cheiri* according to the author’s opinion. Whenever the discussion concerns plants, he intends nothing else to be understood by them than, literally, the plant-flowers of rosemary and others which they commonly call "clove-scented" because of their aroma. At other times, when minerals are being discussed in other places, I warn you to turn your mind toward minerals and consider <term>Anthos</term> <note>original: "Anthos," from the Greek for 'flower'; in alchemy, it refers to the "flower" or essence of a metal</note> to be the red Elixir of gold, and <term>Cheiri</term> indeed to be the white Elixir of silver. That the previous passage discussed minerals is judged by the preceding materials entering the recipe; moreover, where "handfuls" <note>original: "manipulis," a common measurement for herbs</note> are discussed, it can easily be gathered that physicians—both of the <term>Spagyric</term> <note>alchemical medicine practiced by followers of Paracelsus, focused on separating and recombining "essences"</note> and of the other school <note>referring to traditional Galenic medicine</note>—were accustomed to measuring mineral powders by weight, not by handfuls.

Book on Contractions, Treatise 2, Chapter 2.

*Cure for ringing in the ears and deafness, and for dullness of vision, resulting from the noise of artillery* <note>original: "tormentorum," referring to cannons or siege engines</note> *or the sound of bells.*

Let the hanging parts of the ears <note>the earlobes</note> be pricked while in a bath, so that blood flows out. If this does not help, let <term>cupping glasses</term> <note>original: "ventosæ," suction devices used to draw blood or "humors" to the surface of the skin</note> be applied behind the ears. If this again does not help, let a cutting of the vein be made under the tongue. After which there is no [further] remedy.

For affected eyes and weakened vision, let a frequent <term>fomentation</term> <note>a warm, moist medicinal application</note> be made with the flour of vetches <note>a type of legume</note> and barley cooked in water, by holding poultices of this kind over the eyes.

**Gold of Life** <note>original: "Aurum vitae" – a powerful chemical medicine made from gold and mercury, highly valued in the 16th and 17th centuries for its supposed universal healing properties.</note>

Create an amalgam from two parts of cleansed and purified mercury and one part of gold. Let the mercury that is not amalgamated be squeezed out through a goat-skin. Let it be amalgamated again and squeezed out, and repeat this until 7 or 8 ounces of mercury are combined with 1 ounce of gold. Let the amalgam be dissolved in *strong water* <term>Strong water: Nitric acid (aqua fortis), a powerful solvent used in alchemy to dissolve metals</term> made from 2 parts of vitriol <note>original: "vitrioli" – metal sulfates used to create acids</note> and 1 part of saltpeter, and purified with silver plates. Let a portion of 2 ounces be separated by distillation and poured back on again, and do this five times by cohobating <term>Cohobation: The process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from the same solid substance to concentrate its chemical "virtue" or strength</term>. In this way, a very red powder is produced, which is placed in a covered melting pot and left among the glowing coals until it is moderately white-hot, then it is removed. When cooled, it is washed with rose water, dried, and saved for use. The dose of it is: for the young, the weight of half a denarius <note>original: "denarius" – a historical unit of weight used in pharmacy, roughly equivalent to 3 or 4 grams</note>; for the strong and for adults, a whole denarius. It is for obstructions, skin diseases, the venereal disease <note>original: "morbum venereum" – a reference to syphilis, which was often treated with mercury-based preparations during this era</note>, the plague, leprosy, etc. It may be used for the most persistent ulcers if taken by mouth, or it may be mixed into ointments and plasters.

                                                                 *Balsam*

## 28 ON THE CURING OF DISEASES

Oil from egg yolks, as much as is sufficient

Book on Preparations, Treatise 1.
On litharge. <note>Litharge is a secondary mineral form of lead monoxide, commonly used in historical ointments to dry wounds.</note>

### *Cure for Cancer.*
<note>In early modern medicine, "cancer" (or canker) referred to a wide range of persistent, "eating" ulcers and hard tumors, not just the modern clinical definition of carcinoma.</note>

Take: Water glue <note>likely a gelatinous glue made from fish or animal hides</note>, as much as you wish. Reduce it to a liquid by distillation, to which add an equal amount of colcothar <term>colcothar: a reddish-brown iron oxide residue left after the distillation of sulfuric acid</term>. To each 1 pound of this, add 1 and a half ounces of flowers of copper <term>flowers of copper: also known as verdigris, a green pigment/chemical formed by the action of acetic acid on copper plates</term>. Let these be mixed and applied, and let them remain until they putrefy; this usually happens by the fourth day. Next, use a cleansing plaster, as prescribed for the treatment of a malignant growth. After the extraction of the impurities, sprinkle the powder that follows.

### *Powder suitable for the cure of cancer.*

Take: Powder from the juice of celandine.
Feverfew
Centaury, half an ounce of each.
Mummy, 1 ounce. <note>Mummy (mumia) was a standard pharmaceutical ingredient consisting of pulverized embalmed remains or bitumen, believed to have exceptional healing and "stanching" properties.</note>
By binding this to the cancerous ulcer morning and evening, it is excellently healed.
From the *Little Surgery*, Book 2, chapter 7.

### *Cure for a cancerous abscess.*

<margin>Remarkable {</margin> Its cure is threefold. First, the physical, through which hemorrhagic and menstrual flows are to be removed <note>The author suggests that "cancers" were caused by an excess of "black bile" or stagnant blood that should have been purged through menstruation or other flows</note>; second, the surgical, by mortifying the growth with a pain-relieving <note>original: "anodynum"</note> ointment; third, by consolidating and healing the tissue.
1 pound of water glue reduced to a liquid.
Colcothar...

# ON CURING DISEASES
### A most certain cure for cancer and fistulas.

**Take:** 1 ounce of the greatest prepared mercury.
1 measure <note>original: "vertonem" - likely a specific local liquid measure</note> of oil of bricks <note>distilled by heating bricks soaked in oil; used for joint pain and ulcers</note> <insert>3 ounces</insert>
Half a pound of turpentine <insert>see below</insert>
2 ounces of cloves, added through mixing.
Let them be mixed and distilled by means of a water bath <term>balneum: a gentle heating method where the vessel is placed in hot water to ensure even temperature</term>; afterwards, to the distillate let there be added:
Mastic,
Myrrh,
Frankincense, 3 ounces of each.
1 measure of mummy <term>mumia: a substance used in early medicine, often derived from mineral pitch or, historically, processed remains</term>, which is 3 ounces.
All these mixed together should be distilled again through an alembic <term>alembic: a chemical apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used for distilling volatile substances</term> into an oil. To 1 ounce of this, add 1 ounce of the prepared mercury mentioned above in the recipe; it should be understood that the mercury is not to be mixed with the others during the second distillation, but only in the first.

From all this, let a liniment <note>a thin liquid or lotion made with oil for rubbing on the body</note> be made, with which the said ulcers should be anointed. Afterwards, they should be bound with a styptic plaster <term>styptic: a substance used to stop bleeding or contract organic tissue</term>.

This wash, however, must precede the application of the liniment mentioned above.

->**The Wash**<-
**Take:** Half an ounce of alum.
1 ounce of salt.
2 ounces of honey.
1 pound each of wine and water.
Let them be mixed together until dissolved.
The sweet oils of antimony, lead, and mercury provide even better liniments than the one written above.

**Of Gangrene**

### 32 ON THE CURE OF DISEASES

This preparation is good, but the previous one is better. <note>original: "Hæc præparatio bona, prior verò melior."</note>
From the *Book of Preparations: On Silver.*

*Cure for Shingles.* <note>original: "Cinzillæ cura." The term "Cinzilla" is a variation of "Cingulum," referring to Herpes Zoster or shingles, named for the "belt" of blisters it forms around the torso.</note>
The <term>root of the diaphragm|In this context, likely referring to the nerve pathways or the anatomical origin of the pain in the midsection</term> is to be cleansed with royal comfrey, and afterward, bound with <term>oppodeltoch|A medicinal plaster or liniment specifically associated with Paracelsian medicine</term> every twelve hours.

*The formula for Oppodeltoch.*
Take: 1 lb. of Colophony <note>A distilled pine resin, also known as rosin</note>. 
4 oz. each of the powders of Celandine and Orange.
Larch turpentine <note>original: "Visci de botin"</note>, as much as is sufficient to incorporate the ingredients.

From the *Book on Abscesses, Chapter 32.*

*The checking of any offending matter, lest it proceed further into any disease.*

<note>In the original text, a vertical bracket groups the following paragraph.</note>
When pains of the teeth, neck, ears, or shoulders break out, one must purge with the aforementioned root of black hellebore, prepared in the same manner as for <term>gout|A painful form of arthritis</term> or <term>epilepsy|A neurological disorder then often treated with strong purgatives</term>, and in the same dose. Afterward, <term>tincture of Mars|An alchemical preparation of iron</term> is to be used, as in cases of <term>dropsy|The swelling of soft tissues due to the accumulation of excess water</term>. Such purgations can be performed annually—not strictly according to the count of 365 days, but based on the increase or the interval between <term>paroxysms|The sudden recurrence or intensification of symptoms</term>.

From the *Small Book on the Preparation of Hellebore.*

of the known <term>pound</term><note>original: "℔. j." — The symbol ℔ represents a pound weight.</note> 1. Let everything be digested together in <term>dung</term><note>Horse manure was used by alchemists as a natural, steady heat source for gentle "digestion" or heating.</note> for a month, and afterward in ashes for one month, with a slow fire indeed, so that night and day the vapors may ascend and descend. *The Elixir of Salt, a preservative of the body.*

Take salt very well prepared, most white and most pure. Let it be placed in a <term>pelican</term><note>A specialized glass vessel with "arms" that return condensed vapors to the main body, used for continuous circulation.</note>, with a quantity of solvent water that exceeds the weight of the salt sixfold. Let them be digested together in horse dung for one month. Afterward, let the solvent water be separated by distillation and poured on again, and separated as before; and do this so many times until the salt is converted into an oil. Then let an eighth part of the quintessence of gold be added, and let them be digested together in a pelican and in a horse's belly <note>meaning inside a warm pile of manure</note> for four months. Afterward, let them be circulated for a month. Then let another part of <term>circulated wine</term><note>Highly rectified or distilled alcohol.</note> be added, and let them remain in ascension <note>evaporating and condensing</note> for a month still. That time having passed, the **Elixir of Salt** will be completed, of no less virtue in conserving and preserving than the Elixir of Balsam.

->**Elixir of Sweetness.**<-

Quintessence of wine,
Magistery <note>a medicinal preparation made by dissolving and then precipitating a substance, intended to capture its essential virtues</note> of the same, half an ounce of each.

**Coralline Mercury or Red Mercury.**

Mercury is prepared according to the process for its "ladanum" <note>likely a resinous or gummy preparation of mercury</note> and is soaked in rectified vitriol water <note>sulfuric acid solution</note>, as is usually done with spirit of tartar. Finally, it is distilled <note>or evaporated</note> until it turns red like coral. The dose is 1 grain, taken with Mithridate or Theriac <note>famous ancient multi-ingredient "universal antidotes" used as carriers for potent medicines</note> according to the requirements of the disease.

**Or Otherwise.**

Water distilled from egg whites is congealed, and the ground substance is soaked with another water drawn from egg whites and half a part of eggshells through distillation. It is distilled and poured back over repeatedly until it takes on a red and coralline color. Afterward, it is thoroughly soaked in spirit of wine <note>distilled alcohol</note> and digested <note>heated gently</note> until it releases its color into the spirit, which is then separated from the coralline essence. The dose of this is 3 drops, to be drunk in a very fine wine to completely uproot gout <note>original: "podagram"</note> and eradicate venereal disease <note>original: "venereum morbum," often referring to syphilis in this period</note>.

## Cure for Diarrhea through Cachima

Take: As much prepared <term>Cachima|A term used in Paracelsian medicine for mineral dross or an imperfect metallic ore</term> as you wish, as used for dysentery, and a little nutmeg oil to incorporate it, and let it be reduced by a second degree of fire <note>a specific level of heat in early chemistry, stronger than a water bath but not yet a glowing heat</note> into the form of a bolus <note>a large, soft medicinal pill</note>.

Additions can be made.
To 1 dram of this prepared cachima.
3 drams of Theriac <note>original: "Teriacæ"; a famous "universal" herbal jam or electuary containing many ingredients</note>.
Let them be formed into a paste, the dose of which is taken by mouth, from 1 dram to 2 and a half drams, in the morning, around noon, and in the evening. Afterward, for the next three days, this paste is not to be taken. When those days have passed, let it be given again three times in one day as described above. Repeat this process until the third time.
Book of Preparations, on Cachima.

Take: 6 ounces of well-ground cachima.
Half a dram of iron rust <note>original: "Ferruginis ferri"</note>.
Let them be calcined <note>heated to a high temperature to drive off volatile impurities</note> at the second degree of fire for a space of seven hours; afterward, let it be reduced into its alkali <note>original: "alcali"; refers here to the soluble salt or residue obtained from the ashes</note>.

*The Balsam of Hellebore, which I consider to be the*
        *soul extracted from the living plant*

Take the herb and the root of Black Hellebore. Let them be crushed in their own juice <note>literally "in their own water"</note> using the heat of smoke <note>fume heat or a gentle steam bath</note>, in a vessel very well closed and sealed. The dose of the putrefied part should be considered according to its degrees, but the dose of the moisture should be considered according to its quantity.

Hellebore was a powerful but dangerous plant used in ancient and early modern medicine to treat "melancholy" and as a strong purgative.</note> cut into fairly large pieces, with a quarter pound <term>℔; a libra or pound</term> of meat, also cut up, 1 ounce of nutmeg water, and 1 ounce of long pepper water. Pour off the liquid and discard the residue. This broth is to be administered according to the demands of necessity and with respect to the specific person, yet neither more nor less than is appropriate.

->BOOK ONE 45<-

After the metals have been brought into a liquid substance in this manner, and prepared for the separation of their elements—which can hardly happen while they are in a metallic state—for each thing must first be prepared for the specific use to which it is suited. To one part of the aforementioned <term>metallic oil|a concentrated liquid state of a metal, often achieved through dissolution in acids</term>, add two parts of fresh <term>aqua fortis|literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid</term>, and after storing them together in a well-sealed glass vessel of the highest quality, you should place it in horse manure <note>original: "fimum equinum"; horse manure was used by alchemists as a natural, steady source of gentle heat for fermentation or digestion.</note> for a month. Afterwards, you shall distill it with a slow fire throughout, so that the matter is <term>coagulated|thickened or solidified from a liquid state</term> at the bottom and the aqua fortis rises, which you will then distill using a water bath <note>original: "per balneum"; likely referring to a bain-marie or sand bath to ensure even heating.</note>. In this way, two elements are obtained, but not from all metals in the same manner: for from **gold**, earth and water remain in the bath, but air is present in all three of these; the element of fire, however, remains at the bottom, because the substance—or the "tangibility"—of gold is solidified from fire. Therefore, substance corresponds to the substantial. From the **moon** <note>In alchemy, the Moon (Luna) represents the metal Silver.</note>, the element of water will remain at the bottom, and in the bath, the elements of earth and fire. For the substance, or "corporeality" of the moon (so to speak), is taken from the moist and the cold; this is indeed of a fixed nature, so that it cannot be raised <note>Meaning it cannot be easily evaporated or sublimated during the heating process.</note>. From **mercury**, fire remains at the bottom, while earth and water are raised upward. From **Venus** <note>The planet Venus represents the metal Copper.</note>, fire also remains at the bottom, and both earth and water are found in the bath. From **Saturn** <note>The planet Saturn represents the metal Lead.</note>, the element of earth remains at the bottom, while fire and water are held in the bath. From **Jupiter** <note>The planet Jupiter represents the metal Tin.</note>, air remains at the bottom, and fire, water, and earth are raised from it.

It should therefore be noted that air manifests as a "body" <note>meaning a tangible or physical residue</note> in Jupiter, but in no other metal whatsoever. Although

<note>original: DE CVRANDIS MORBIS</note>

Though some part of the air may ascend even with the other elements, being mixed with them in an inseparable way, it is nevertheless not "corporeal air," but rather something concurring and adhering to the others inseparably.

Now let us come to the remainders or residues. Let the corporeal element, which remained at the bottom, be reduced into an oil by means of fresh strong water <term>strong water: *aqua fortis*, a highly corrosive mineral acid, usually nitric acid, used in alchemy to dissolve metals</term> and a bath <note>likely a *balneum Mariae* or water bath for gentle heating</note>, and this element will be perfected: keep it aside.

You shall separate the others in this manner by using a sand bath <term>sand bath: *balneum arenae*, a laboratory technique where a vessel is placed in heated sand to provide uniform high temperatures</term>. Place them in the sand, pressing with a light fire; first, indeed, the water will be elevated, and afterward the fire, which is distinguished from the water by its color; this occurs where these two—namely fire and water—had settled. If, however, the elements of earth and water had remained at the bottom, the water ascends first, and afterward the earth. If earth and fire had settled at the bottom, the earth ascends first, then the fire is elevated. But if water, fire, and earth had remained at the bottom together, the water ascends first, the fire next, and finally the earth. These must be kept in specific glass vessels, each one separately in its own nature—as the hot and dry derived from the sun, without any other property; and similarly the cold and moist, the cold and dry, and so on for the others. One must not be ignorant of the fact that the corrosive nature of the strong water must be extracted <note>meaning the acid must be neutralized or removed so the medicine is safe to use</note>, as we have set forth in the book on the *quintessence* <note>The "Fifth Essence," the purest part of a substance</note>.

Take one pound of talc, garnet, cobalt, or another [mineral], and the same amount of <term>saltpeter<note>Also known as nitre, a key ingredient in many alchemical solvents and explosives.</note></term>. Grind them until they are small, burn them together, and draw them through an <term>alembic<note>The top part of a still, used to condense vapors into liquid.</note></term> out of the <term>cucurbit<note>The bottom "gourd-shaped" vessel of a still where the substance is heated.</note></term>. Save whatever has ascended. Grind what remains at the bottom and dissolve it in <term>aqua fortis<note>literally "strong water"; a powerful solvent, usually nitric acid, used to dissolve silver and other metals.</note></term> into a liquid. Pour the previously separated water onto this and distill it into an oil, just as you have heard regarding the metals. You shall separate the elements of these using the same process. Thus, golden <term>marcasite<note>original: "marcasita." In early chemistry, this referred generally to any mineral or ore that looked metallic or contained sulfur.</note></term> is to be treated in place of gold, and silver marcasite in place of silver. Lead-like marcasite is treated as Saturn <note>Saturn was the alchemical name for lead.</note>, copper-like as Venus, talc-like as Jupiter <note>Jupiter was the alchemical name for tin.</note>, mercurial as Mercury, and so forth.
This process is sufficient for every kind of marcasite.

## Separation of the elements
## from stones.

Take a stone that is very well ground, to which you shall add twice as much native sulfur. When all are mixed together, placed in a <term>luted<note>Sealed with "lute," a paste of clay and other materials used to make vessels airtight.</note></term> cauldron within an <term>athanor<note>A furnace designed to maintain a steady, uniform heat for long periods of time.</note></term>, let them be urged with fire for four hours so that the sulfur is entirely consumed. Afterward, let what remains be washed of dregs and sulfur. Let this stone-lime be dried and dissolved in aqua fortis, and proceed with it as we said before regarding metals. <margin>Analogy of Stones to Metals</margin> Thus, stones are compared to metals: clear gems that are neither white nor dark are to be compared to gold. But white, blue, and sea-green gems are compared to the Moon <note>The Moon was the alchemical name for silver.</note>. Common stones are compared to other metals: Alabaster to Saturn [lead], marble to iron, flint to Jupiter [tin], and those that are fragile, non-solid, and spongy to Mercury.

48 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.
->*The separation of the elements from oily substances.*<-

Take oily woods, roots, seeds, fruits, and similar materials, whether they are crushed, ground, or broken down in any other way. These should be wrapped in linen cloth, tied up, and placed in horse manure <note>original: "fimum equinum"; manure was used by early chemists as a source of steady, gentle heat to encourage fermentation or decay</note> so that they may completely rot, which happens faster to some materials than others. Once they have rotted, place them in a gourd-shaped vessel <note>a cucurbit, the bottom part of a still</note> and pour in enough common strong water <note>original: "aquae fortis communis"; likely a mineral acid or a strong solvent used to break down the plant matter</note> so that it stands four finger-widths above the material. Then, distill it in a sand bath <note>a method of heating a vessel evenly by nesting it in warm sand</note> as much as can be made to rise. All the elements will rise except for the earth; these are distinguished by their colors. First, the "burning water" <note>original: "ardens aqua"; the volatile spirit associated with the element of Fire</note> rises, then the air, then the water, and finally the fire <note>in this context, "fire" refers to the final heavy oils</note>, while the earth remains at the bottom. <margin>This section outlines the basic distillation procedure for extracting the "four elements" from plant matter.</margin> Pure oils, however, do not require rotting; their elements are separated simply through distillation alone and are recognized by their distinct colors. The same should be done with liquid resins, such as pitch, resin, turpentine, gum, and similar substances. However, solid resins must be prepared beforehand, as described below.

->*The separation of the elements from solid resins.*<-

Take very finely ground sulfur and cook it in twice its weight of linseed oil until it becomes a "liver" <term>hepar: a chemical compound, often a sulfide, named for its dark, liver-like color</term>. Grind this, seal it in a bladder, and place it in horse manure to rot for four weeks. Afterward, distill it slowly through a still-head <note>an alembic</note> over a bare fire. First of all, the air will rise...

BOOK ONE. 49
->...<- air and water appear in various pale colors. After the fire has been increased, the fire will rise upward, while the earth remains at the bottom. Each is separated into its own proper <term>complexion</term> <note>In the context of early chemistry and medicine, "complexion" refers to the specific balance of qualities—hot, cold, moist, or dry—that defines a substance's nature.</note>, perfect and without any mixing.

*The separation of the elements from herbs.*

Take sage, or another herb, and crush its leaves; then set them to rot <note>original: "putrefaciendum." In alchemy, "putrefaction" was considered a vital first step to break down the physical structure of a plant to release its spiritual or elemental essence.</note> stored in a bladder <note>A "vesica" or bladder was a common vessel used for fermentation and gentle distillation.</note>. Afterward, you shall distill it by means of a "horse's belly" <note>original: "ventrem equinum." This was a traditional alchemical technique where a vessel was buried in a pile of fermenting horse manure to provide a steady, gentle, and natural heat for long periods.</note>. First, the element of fire will rise until the colors are changed. Then, a thick water follows, and afterward the greater part of the earth, though some portion of it remains fixed at the bottom. 

Distill this mixed water in the sun for six days, and afterward, by means of a bath <note>Likely a "balneum Mariae" or water bath, used to provide a more controlled heat than a direct flame.</note>, the element of water will rise first; there is little of it, and it is perceived by taste. After the color changes, the element of fire rises until the taste is also altered. Finally, a part of the earth is raised upward—though only a very small amount—and the other part of the earth remains at the bottom. The air, however, is mixed with all the others. The same is done with the remaining "airy" and "aquatic" herbs, in which the air rises first, then the water, and finally the earth, etc., just as in the process described for Sage.

*Separations of the elements from fleshy substances.*

In those bodies that live by blood, such as living creatures, the predominant element is both the most abundant and the most excellent. For example, in fish the predominant element is water; in worms it is fire; and in edible meats it is air, and so on.

*From fish.*
Let the putrefied <note>In alchemical practice, "putrefaction" was a deliberate stage of breaking down a substance through gentle heat and moisture to prepare it for transformation.</note> fish be best distilled by means of a horse’s belly <note>original: "ventrem equinum"; a traditional alchemical term for a digestion vessel buried in a warm, fermenting heap of horse manure, which provided a steady, gentle heat.</note>, and the water will mostly rise. Repeat this putrefaction and distillation until no more water will rise. Afterward, distill what remains through a sand bath <note>original: "per arenam"; a method of heating a vessel by placing it in a container of sand, which distributes heat more evenly than a naked flame.</note>; then finally the fire will rise in the form of oil, while the earth remains at the bottom. In this way, the entire substance of the fish is separated into its elements. The same applies to the rest.

*Separations of elements from watery, juicy, or aqueous things.*

♃ <note>This symbol refers to Jupiter, which in an alchemical and medical context usually represents the metal tin or specific preparations associated with the planet Jupiter.</note>. Take urine or another excrement and drive it through distillation; the water, air, and earth will rise together, but the fire remains in the bottom. Afterward, mix everything back together and distill again, repeating this four times in the same manner. In the fourth distillation, the water will rise first, then the air and fire, while the earth will remain at the bottom.

♃ <note>Jupiter/Tin symbol.</note>. Finally, place the air and fire in a special vessel and set it in a cold place. Certain [underlined] small pointed crystals <note>original: "pinnacidiola"; literally "little wings" or "fins," referring to the needle-like shape of the resulting crystals.</note> will freeze, similar to those that are made [/underlined] from saltpeter <note>original: "sale petræ"; literally "salt of the rock," known today as potassium nitrate.</note>, and these are the element of fire. Although they may also freeze during the distillation process, yet in the cold...

Dung, <term>vitriol</term> <note>A historical term for sulfate minerals, such as iron or copper sulfate.</note>, <term>tartar</term> <note>A potassium salt found in wine barrels, frequently used in alchemical experiments.</note>, alums, salts, and other things of that kind which are "juicy" should be distilled through ash until they no longer wish to rise. [The following four lines are underlined in the original]
It should be noted in this place that, although the four elements have been separated, there nevertheless remain four hidden elements in the earth as if they were fixed; for example, from vitriol remains the <term>dead head</term> <note>original: "caput mortuum"; the useless chemical residue remaining after distillation or sublimation.</note>. Separate the two prior elements that ascended, and the water will rise again, while the fire will remain at the bottom. Understand the same regarding tartar and salts. There are many more separations of liquids which we shall explain in the section on transmutations. It should also be noted that in corrosive substances, "earth" predominates over the other elements; therefore, their elements must be separated through ashes and sublimation.
