<meta>This page continues a technical discussion on the "Archidoxes," a foundational work by Paracelsus regarding the extraction of the "mystery of nature" through chemical separation. The previous section likely detailed the separation of the element of Fire.</meta>

FIRST BOOK. 53

...while through circulation it is transformed into a watery substance that increases day by day, which you shall separate as you will soon hear. Let it rot in horse manure <note>original: "equino fimo"; decaying horse dung was a standard laboratory heat source in the Renaissance, providing a gentle, steady temperature similar to a modern incubator.</note> for four weeks. Afterward, you shall distill it using a water bath and ashes, just as was described regarding the element of fire. More will be said elsewhere concerning its specific powers.

### Separation from water.

Place the substance in a glass vessel that is completely full, without even a tiny bit of empty space, closed with the **seal of Hermes** <term>seal of Hermes: an airtight seal created by heating the glass neck of a flask until it melts and can be twisted shut.</term>. It should be kept in very hot sand for one month, so that it receives constant heat as if it were boiling—though it cannot actually boil because the vessel is entirely full. When this time has passed, let it rot for four weeks. Then, after opening the vessel, let it be distilled through a **three-beaked alembic** <note>original: "alembicum trium rostrorum"; a specialized distillation head with three separate delivery tubes, allowing the chemist to collect different "elements" or fractions at the same time.</note>. By this method, the three elements are separated, and the "earth" remains at the bottom.

### Separation from earth.

The process for "earth" is to be followed just as with "water," except for the distillation, which must be performed in the same way as was described for "fire," and conducted by that same route.

### The stictic plaster, and by what reasoning it is made attractive.

<margin>A vertical bracket marks this paragraph in the original text, highlighting its importance.</margin>
To any **stictic plaster** <term>A "stictic" plaster was a specific type of healing bandage intended to "stitch" or close a wound and draw out impurities</term>, add whatever substances possess **attractive powers**. These include **Asphalt** and **amber** <note>original: carabe</note>, which draw to themselves wood, straw, and fragments of cloth; the **Magnet**, which draws iron; **Tin** and **tinctures of Amethyst**, which attract copper; **Alabaster**, which attracts lead and stones; **gum**, **turpentine**, and **Asphalt**, which also attract fragments of flesh and bone; and **Serpentine** <note>Likely the mineral serpentine stone, though sometimes used for herbs</note>, which is attractive to all things. Even though these substances have their own specific powers of attraction, when they are joined together, mixed into ointments, and placed over wounds, they nonetheless attract all things at once.
*Small Surgery*, Book 1, Chapter 5.

->*The purging of epilepsy, the falling sickness, and its various types.*<-

Take: Roots of Black Hellebore <note>A potent and toxic plant traditionally used as a drastic purgative to treat madness and "falling sickness."</note> which have purple flowers—as much as you wish and as fresh as possible. Once they have been macerated in <term>burning wine</term><note>original: "vino ardente," referring to distilled spirits or brandy</note>, let them be distilled first with a slow fire, and afterwards with the heat increased until all the liquid has risen. Afterward, let the "burning wine" be separated from the substance by a <term>bath</term><note>original: "balneum," likely a bain-marie or water bath for gentle heating</note> using a lighter fire. In that which remains, let there be dissolved and added as much white sugar as seems sufficient. The dose of this is three grains, taken with <term>mead</term><note>original: "aqua mulsa," a mixture of water and honey</note> or common water in which <term>horse-tongue</term><note>original: "hippoglossum," likely the plant Ruscus hypoglossum, used in ancient medicine</note> has been boiled.

->*Common Cure for Erysipelas*<-

The nature of this medicine is such that it does not take effect before three weeks have passed.

## Cure for fistulas.

**Take Orpiment,** <note>a yellow mineral consisting of arsenic trisulfide</note> 1 ounce.
**Calcined Tartar,** <note>potassium carbonate produced by heating the crusty sediment from wine vats</note> 3 ounces.
Arranged in layers, let them be reduced in the **fourth degree of fire** <note>the highest level of heat in early chemical practice</note> for 24 hours; in this way the material was made. Afterwards, let it be removed, ground up, and boiled in water; a white powder will settle to the bottom, which is the prepared orpiment. This, stored in glass or placed upon marble in a damp place, turns into an oil to be injected into fistulas with a syringe; or, threads may be soaked in this oil, or the powder may be sprinkled in.

## *The cure for a sooty abscess.*
<note>In early medicine, a "sooty" (fuliginosi) abscess was thought to be caused by thick, dark vapors or "sooty" humors trapped under the skin.</note>

The cure for this is threefold: through maturation,
cleansing, and consolidation <note>the process of closing and healing a wound</note>. These and
similar abscesses should not be opened with surgical instruments, but allowed to open on their own,
applying only ripening agents <note>substances intended to bring an abscess to a "head"</note>.

*Ripening Agent.*

Take 1 ounce of oil of bricks <term>Oil of Bricks: Also known as "Oil of Philosophers," this was produced by heating bricks soaked in olive oil until a medicinal liquid was distilled</term>.
Half an ounce <note>the symbol ß represents "semis," meaning one-half</note> of strained opopanax <term>Opopanax: A gum resin derived from the "Hercules-all-heal" plant, used to soften hard swellings</term>.
Let a plaster be made over hot coals: the opopanax
is strained through vinegar, then a mass is made
and hardened.

*Cleansing Agent.*

Take 1 ounce each of honey and self-heal <note>original: "prunellæ," likely referring to the herb Prunella vulgaris, commonly used for wounds</note>.
Round birthwort <note>a plant used since antiquity to treat skin conditions and infections</note>
and hepatic aloe <note>a dark grade of aloe used for its purifying properties</note>, 1 ounce each.
Let a mixture be made in the manner of a plaster, which should
be used after the abscess has been opened.

*Healing Agent.*

Take egg yolks

# BOOK ONE. 69
## Frostbite of the limbs, and its cure.

**Rx.** <note>original: "℞," the symbol for "Recipe" or "Take."</note> **Long pepper.**
**Cardamom.**
**Grains of paradise**, <term>of each 1 drachm.</term> <note>The Latin "ana" signifies equal parts of the preceding ingredients.</note>
**Euphorbium**, <term>2 drachms.</term> <note>Euphorbium is the dried sap of a succulent plant, used historically for its intense, burning heat.</note>
**Mastic**, <term>1 and a half drachms.</term>
They should be ground in a mortar, and two measures of the **urine of boys**, or of some **choleric man**, should be poured over them; <note>In the theory of the four humors, a "choleric" person was naturally hot and dry, and their bodily fluids were thought to carry those warming properties.</note> let them be boiled until an eighth part has evaporated. Let the mixture be strained through a cloth: with what remains, let the frozen limb be anointed three times every day. The dead part will be separated from the living <note>original: "a vino," likely an OCR error for "a vivo" (the living), as the context refers to the separation of necrotic tissue.</note>, and the living part should be treated with wound-healing ointments. Truly, after a healthy limb has been anointed with this ointment near a fire, it will be protected from the frost for that day.

Similarly, a limb is preserved from the frost by wrapping it in **double or triple soaked paper** with a linen cloth placed over it, for it does not allow the air and wind to penetrate.

If the frost has penetrated to the internal organs so that death is imminent, let <term>Aqua Vitae</term> <note>Literally "Water of Life," referring to high-proof distilled spirits or brandy.</note> mixed with **camphor** and a small amount of **theriac** be given as a drink, <note>Theriac was a complex, prestigious "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients.</note> after which a sweat should be induced. It would be even better to use a **noble wine** boiled with **ginger** and **sweet flag**. <note>original: "calamo aromatico."</note>

Also **Aqua Vitae** with aromatic spices and especially **saffron**: each of these, when drunk, protects those crossing frozen mountains from the frost. Excessive cold is sometimes followed by sudden death, or leprosy, or at the very least, <term>scabies</term> <note>Here referring generally to skin eruptions or irritations caused by the cold.</note> etc.

->**From the Great Surgery, Part 1, Book 3, Chapter 12.**<-

->BOOK ONE. 75<-

**Hydrophorbia** <note>This term likely refers to a specific type of skin eruption or abscess associated with the use of Euphorbium or a condition mimicking its effects.</note> is a distinct <term>abscess <note>Original: "apostema." A localized collection of pus or a swelling.</note></term> originating from the salt of the <term>three principles <note>Original: "trium primorum." These are Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, the fundamental building blocks of matter in Paracelsian alchemy.</note></term>. It is one of the indicators of leprosy; it is a red, narcotic salt, and is in some way leprous.

The cure is twofold: one part is achieved through the **liquor of the horizon** <note>An alchemical preparation, possibly a solvent or a highly purified "universal" liquid.</note>, by which nature must be strengthened; the other is through the following water:

*Extinguishing burns.*

Burns caused by niter <note>original: "à nitro"; niter refers to potassium nitrate or similar salts, which can cause chemical burns</note> are extinguished by purified and dissolved vitriol <note>original: "vitriolum"; a term for metallic sulfates, often used in early medicine for their astringent properties</note>. For burns caused by sulfur and resins, use "virgin's milk" <note>original: "lac virginis"; an alchemical term for a milky-white suspension or emulsion, in this case derived from sulfur</note> extracted from sulfur.

Let the area be bound with cloths soaked in these kinds of liquids; do not allow the site to become dry, but let it always be kept moist.

182      ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES

...previously made from calcined tartar <note>Potassium bitartrate, a salty crust found in wine barrels, used frequently in early modern chemistry</note>, completely drawn out. *Liquor of Tartar.*

Let tartar from white wine be calcined <note>heated to a high temperature to drive off volatile substances</note> until it reaches whiteness, and by the process of oil of vitriol <note>Sulfuric acid</note> let it be distilled into a liquor. Afterward, using an upright gourd-shaped vessel, let the phlegm <note>the watery, tasteless part of a distillate</note> be separated from the oil. Next, let this be distilled through ashes <note>using a sand or ash bath to provide indirect, even heat</note> into an oil of golden color, for the purpose of curing ulcers, especially those that take their origin from the venereal disease <note>Syphilis, often called the "French Disease" in this period</note>.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of "Book One" in this volume. It transitions from the previous page's discussion on herbal wines and salts for uterine health to the alchemical preparation of gemstones and metallic medicines, specifically mercury.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

Following the process now established for crystals across three preparations—namely, **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to change its physical or chemical properties, often reducing it to powder.</note>, **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance so it turns directly from a solid into a vapor and then cools back into a solid, used here for purification.</note>, and **distillation**—these transparent gems can be prepared, each according to its own specific effects: **Garnets**, **Sapphires**, **Emeralds**, **Hyacinths**, and **Rubies**, with their additional ingredients as shown below in the *Book of Preparations, concerning gems.*

*Ladanum of mercury.* <note>original: "Ladanum mercury" — while Ladanum usually refers to a plant resin, here it refers to a purified, oil-like alchemical extract of mercury.</note>

Let mercury be purified as well as possible by any superior method, and let it be sublimated with equal weights of **vitriol** <note>Sulfate minerals, often used as strong acidic agents in alchemy.</note> and **saltpeter** <note>Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in chemical reactions.</note>, having first been ground in a mortar with a wooden pestle using the strongest vinegar; the more often this is done, the better. Afterward, let it be sublimated again with calcined alum and saltpeter; then finally it will be thoroughly purified. Let its powder be drenched with **burning water** <note>original: "aqua ardente" — a term for distilled alcohol or brandy</note>, and let it be drawn off [distilled]; let more be poured on and drawn off until there remains at the bottom a substance in the form of a very white oil. Let it be dried, crushed into powder, and dissolved upon a marble slab in a damp place. Finally, let it be dried out and kept for use. It is called the **Theriac of Metals**. <term>Theriac: Traditionally a complex "universal" antidote; here, the author claims this mercury preparation serves as a supreme medicine derived from the mineral kingdom.</term> Its dose is one grain taken with theriac or mithridate <note>Another famous historical semi-mythical herbal antidote</note>, and a very excellent wine.

*Oil of Litharge.*
Let it be drenched in distilled vinegar and digested in a warm place for some time; let it be poured off, and another fresh portion of vinegar added, digested, and poured off; and this should be done as many times as necessary until the vinegar is no longer colored. Let all this collected colored vinegar be distilled in a water bath <note>original: "balneo" — the "bain-marie" or water bath, used to apply gentle, indirect heat</note> until an oil remains at the bottom. This is a most effective remedy for ulcerations, burns, tumors, and pains arising from excessive heat. *Oil of Litharge.*
Take: Alum, vinegar, and litharge <term>litargirium: lead monoxide, a common byproduct of silver refining used in plasters and ointments</term>. Cook them together until the litharge turns white. *From the Minor Surgery, Book 3, Chapter 3.*

### The killing of worms, whether in wounds, or in a whitlow of the fingers.

<note>A "whitlow" (original: *panaritio*) is a painful, deep infection of the fingertip. In the 16th century, these were often thought to be caused by microscopic or literal "worms" eating the flesh.</note>

Take an earthworm, either small or large according to the nature of the ailment. Bind this worm while still alive over the finger or the wounds, and leave it there—not tied so tightly as to cut off circulation—until it dies of its own accord. Together with it, the worms of the disease will also die through a certain sympathy <note>The concept of "sympathy" was a cornerstone of Paracelsian medicine, suggesting that like affects like and that a living creature could "draw out" a disease of a similar nature</note>. If one worm is not enough, use several.
From the *Book on Worms*, chapter 9.

(two drams)</term> of spirit of wine, and they are to be digested together in warm ashes for 24 days. *The Mastery of Pearls.* <note>original: "Magisterium vnionum." In alchemy, a "magistery" (magisterium) is a preparation that extracts the essential power of a substance while keeping its full medicinal properties intact. "Uniones" refers specifically to large, high-quality pearls.</note>

Let them digest in it for a month, and in this manner they will be dissolved into water. Let the vinegar be separated by distillation in a water bath; you will find the pearls at the bottom of the vessel, dissolved into a liquid state. This is the mastery of pearls or "uniones." Corals, however, must first be ground and calcined <note>Heated to a high temperature to be reduced to a powder.</note> from the beginning with saltpeter <note>original: "sale nitro."</note>; afterward, they are to be prepared and dissolved just like gemstones.

*The Mastery of Marcasites.* <note>original: "Marcasitā." Marcasite was a general term in early chemistry for various metallic ores and pyrites, often thought to be the "seed" or "immature" form of metals.</note>

Take: Very finely ground marcasite. Pour over it "solvent water" <note>original: "aqua soluente," likely referring to a strong acid or a specialized alchemical solvent.</note> so that it floats four finger-widths above the powder. Let it be dissolved, and afterward let it putrefy <note>A process of slow digestion or fermentation in a warm, moist environment.</note> for one month. Then let it be distilled and separated, as we previously taught regarding metals.

*The Mastery of Fatty Substances.*

Take: As much as you wish of well-ground amber <note>original: "Carabis," a term derived from the Arabic word for yellow amber.</note>, and a sufficient amount of "circulated spirit" <note>original: "circulati." This refers to alcohol that has been repeatedly distilled in a closed vessel so that the vapors return to the liquid, a process meant to "exalt" or purify the spirit.</note>. Let them digest in a flask <note>original: "stacco," likely a reference to a specific shape of distillation vessel or a "storta" (retort).</note> over ashes for six days. Afterward, let the circulated spirit be distilled from it and poured back over it; let this be done as many times as necessary until oil is found at the bottom. This is the mastery of amber <note>original: "arabis," likely a scribe's error for "carabis."</note>.

*The Mastery of Resinous Substances.*

Take: Turpentine, gum, or resin, which, being sealed <note>original: "lutatum," referring to a vessel sealed with "lute," a specialized alchemical paste made of clay or dung used to make glass containers airtight.</note>...

*Oil of Glass or of Gems.*

## **The Practice of Extraction**

The body of gold must be stripped of its metallic nature through "corruption" <note>In alchemy, corruption refers to the process of breaking down a substance's physical structure to release its hidden medicinal or spiritual essence.</note>, so that it can no longer withstand the strike of a hammer. This is done using salt water. The residue is then washed with fresh water, and the tincture <note>A concentrated liquid extract of the "soul" of the metal.</note> is finally drawn out using spirit of wine <term>Spirit of wine|High-proof distilled alcohol or ethanol</term>. Lastly, the spirit is evaporated away from the tincture, which will remain settled at the bottom of the vessel.

## **Preparation of the Salt Water**

It is made from liquid salt, or from salt that has been liquefied, which exists naturally and of its own accord without the need for the boiling or artificial preparations usually used to create salts. Let it be liquefied several times; once powdered, it should be mixed with the juice of radish roots and stirred until it dissolves, then distilled.

This distillate should be mixed with bloodstone <note>original: "sanguineæ." This refers to hematite or perhaps heliotrope, used here in its "green" or unrefined state.</note> while it is still raw, and the mixture distilled again five times. In this liquid, thin sheets of gold <note>original: "laminæ solis," literally "sheets of the sun," the common alchemical name for gold.</note>—which have first been purified using antimony—will dissolve into a powder. This powder should be carefully washed with pure, fresh, distilled water until all the biting sharpness of the salt is removed. It is easily washed because the substance does not mix deeply; instead, the water separates so that a "sweet" <note>In this context, "sweet" means neutral or free from corrosive acids.</note> powder remains, which is consistent in its own nature, etc.

## **Extraction of the Spirit of Wine**

**Take** <note>The symbol ♃ usually represents Jupiter/Tin, but in medical recipes, it is often a shorthand for *Recipe*, meaning "Take."</note> a certain measure of the most noble wine, white or red, which has not been tampered with by any artifice. Place this into a circulatory vessel <term>Circulatory vessel|A glass alchemical vessel, such as a "pelican," designed so that vapor rises, condenses, and flows back into the base for continuous refinement.</term> of a size appropriate to the amount of material, so that it may be freely circulated...

*Clarification of the more obscure passages contained within the practical application.*

The greatest difficulty lies in the "corruption" <note>In alchemy, "corruption" refers to the process of breaking down a substance's physical form to release its inner essence or "soul"</note> of gold. For there is a common opinion that this can hardly be achieved by art, nor conquered by any fire. However, our author <note>Referring to Paracelsus</note> teaches in this passage that it is done with "water of salt." Gold can be calcined <note>reduced to powder through heat</note> or turned into dust by Mercury or the fumes of Saturn <note>lead</note>—though this is of little use—and also by *aqua regia* <note>original Latin: "royal water," a powerful mixture of acids capable of dissolving gold</note>. This powder or lime returns to its former state—that is, into a malleable metal body—once it is subjected to the bellows and fire. Paracelsus by no means considers this simple pulverization to be true "corruption." 

Instead, he expressly suggests to us that the body of gold, which is the most solid among all metals, must be "corrupted" in such a way that it no longer looks like or remains a metal, but is removed entirely from its metallic nature. He teaches that this is done with "water of salt," though he speaks quite obscurely. This is so that this most excellent <term>arcanum|A hidden, powerful medical or alchemical secret</term> is not made known to the unworthy, who are enemies of nature as much as they are of art. 

Nonetheless, as far as I am able, I will reveal this salt to the "sons of art" <note>a traditional term for dedicated students of alchemy</note> and those who study the mysteries. In the bowels of the earth and in the mountains, a salt of nature is hidden, cooked by natural means, which is called "stony" <note>likely referring to saltpeter or nitre</note> and is not made solid by any human art. Once it is dug out and melted by fire, it sometimes acquires powers that are highly destructive to gold—especially if it is afterwards dissolved in the juice of radish roots.

200    **ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES**

In this place, many self-important critics <note>original: *nasutuli*, literally "those with a nose," meaning people who think themselves clever or snooty</note>, rather than true philosophers, are often prone to hesitate over whether the discussion concerns the vegetable radish root, or some kind of mineral. Their doubtful minds will not delay me from understanding these matters strictly literally. For in this passage, we must consider not so much the degree of **sharpness** <note>original: *acuitatis*, referring to the chemical potency or acidity</note> as the **dissolving of the prescribed salt**, although both must be considered by us. 

Lull <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1316), a major figure in medieval alchemy and logic</note> says that the <term>solvents</term> <note>original: *menstrua*; in alchemy, a solvent used to dissolve solids</note> of the philosophers are sharpened by certain vegetables. Leaving those aside, let us return to the dissolving of the salt. What common man, however humble, would doubt that any salt dissolved in ordinary water, or other fresh water, is weakened? It is very well known to the most experienced that the more often this is done, the milder the salt becomes, losing more of its sharpness; consequently, it is rendered less corrosive. 

From this, it follows that the salt is taught to be dissolved in sharper liquids and from more astringent <note>original: *ponticioribus*, a technical term for a harsh, sour, or puckering taste</note> vegetables, which are by no means lacking in their own natural corrosive salt. I have no doubt that this very thing should be understood regarding the **blood-red plant** <note>original: *sanguinea*, likely referring to bloodroot or a similar plant used for its red sap, though some interpreted it as a mineral</note> **while it is green**; even if it seems otherwise to some—indeed, to many—who wish this to be understood as referring to minerals, and who turn their attention to **vitriol** <note>a term for metal sulfates, often green or blue, used as powerful chemical agents</note>. 

The passage is clear in itself; by these words (**while it is green**), he implies that the juice of such a vegetable must be extracted at the time when it is still green: as if he were saying, there is no reason for you to let it dry out or decay. There can be no suspicion regarding vitriol, since it is always "green," and although it sometimes grows pale depending on the qualities of the places from which it is mined—tending toward a blue color, yellow, or white—nevertheless, its greenness, more or less

<summary>The author argues for a literal interpretation of using fresh vegetable juices (like radish or "sanguinea") as solvents for salts in chemical preparations, rather than substituting minerals like vitriol.</summary>

...is perceived to be present in greater or lesser amounts. It should also be considered that the author spoke of a "bloody" <note>original: "sanguineam" (feminine form)</note> substance, not a "bloody" <note>original: "sanguineum" (neuter/masculine form)</note> one, in order to hint at an herb, rather than a vegetable or some other thing. Therefore, best of readers, seek an herb endowed with no less sharpness than the radish itself—wherever you may find it—which is called "bloody." The specified salt should be dissolved in the juices of this herb and the radish, and sharpened so it has greater strength and power for breaking down <note>original: "corrumpendi"; in alchemy, this refers to breaking a metal down to its primary essence</note> gold.

We now come to the understanding of "cold distillation," yet the author did not say the distillation itself is cold, but rather that it is "coldly completed." This means it is performed with the most temperate heat in comparison to other distillations which are commonly used in other extractions. He thinks a sign should be noted in the **helmet** <term>Helmet (gallea): The top part of a distilling apparatus (alembic) where vapors collect and condense.</term> of the distilling vessel, where rays, filaments, or droplets of the bodily moisture appear. For it is necessary that the spirit be invisible while it ascends, and not be perceivable until it has thickened into its "vehicle" <note>The "body" or liquid carrier of the spirit</note> through the action of the cold. Indeed, it is necessary for our chemical spirits to have a body in which they are enclosed; otherwise, they would escape our hands and our power. Yet, this body is so rare <note>thin or subtle</note> that unless it is restrained by some vessel, it would vanish entirely.

**Alcohol** <term>Alcool: In this historical context, "alcohol" refers to an incredibly fine, "impalpable" powder produced by chemical resolution, not the liquid spirit we recognize today.</term> is the name given to a powder that is not crushed by a pestle or in a mortar, but is produced solely through chemical resolutions. It remains to explain what the author wished to keep hidden under an enigma. At this **Lydian stone** <term>Lydian stone: A touchstone used to test the purity of gold; here used metaphorically for a crucial test or a point of failure for the uninitiated.</term> (you who have translated Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note> in this place) you have stumbled. This is evident, since the goal of the entire business consists in that very thing: namely, in that **graduation** <term>Graduation: A process intended to exalt the qualities of a metal, such as its color, density, or purity.</term>. This is the enigma: (that it is not at all a matter of the **fifth essence** <term>Fifth Essence (Quintessence): The purest, most concentrated medicinal or spiritual force extracted from a substance.</term>...

...it allows itself, that is, in 2. 4. 0. <note>original: "2. 4. 0." — these numbers refer to stages of alchemical multiplication explained later in the text</note> but if one should presume to proceed further, it is burned. Listen, I pray you. Have you ever seen the **fifth essence**? <term>quintessence: the purest, most concentrated medicinal part of a substance, believed to be the "spirit" of the matter</term> You are even ignorant of the process for preparing it. Do you not know it is made through **circulation**? <term>circulation: a chemical process where a liquid is heated so it rises as vapor and then condenses and falls back down repeatedly in a closed vessel</term> What else is Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, a famous 16th-century physician and alchemist</note> striving to hint to the "sons of his teaching" than that, through a multiplication that is always doubled, the circle is driven into infinity, until it rests in unity. 

This is clear through the first number, 2. 4. 8. 16. 32. 64. 128. 256. 512. and O <note>The letter O or a zero represents the "circle" or completion</note>, which circular mark designates an innumerable motion, ending through fatigue in the character of rest—that is, the sign of **fixation** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat</term>—and unity. Do not understand this place in the German copy any differently (which is doubled five times, that is, five times twice twenty-four). <note>The author is critiquing a specific mathematical error in a German translation of Paracelsus</note> See, I pray you, by what reasoning of their own they were led astray when they attempted to explain these numbers. 

2. 4. 0. They did not notice among the meaningful marks of arithmetic that the points 2. 4. do not denote 24, but 2 and 4, the beginning of a binary or doubled multiplication. Hence they corrupted the mind of the author, which was proposed to us through an elegant series of speech as an enigma, and they perverted it into a chimerical fantasy, signifying nothing except their own ideas. Rather they should have said: "which is doubled through 2. 4. etc., ending in 0." This should have been opened to us and to every individual by those skilled in the mystical matters of the art and the writings of Paracelsus. Or they should have interpreted it in this manner: "which is doubled twice, that is twice times two is four, etc." and in O, rolling the circle, it returns to unity, by which the **fixation** of the graduated thing is signified; not that with which gold is endowed

### Recipe for Caesar’s or King’s Water

**Take:** Best **vitriol**, <term>vitriol: metal sulfates, often iron or copper, used as a source of acid</term> one pound [℔. j.]; **saltpeter**, one pound [℔. j.]; **burnt alum**, half a pound [℔. ß.]. Let a dissolving water be made according to the art, and into that water you should throw one pound [℔. j.] of **sal ammoniac**. <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used in alchemy to make acids more powerful</term> Then, it should be purified by throwing in thin plates of silver. For if the sal ammoniac were distilled together with the other ingredients, there would be a danger of the vessel breaking, on account of the violence of such a salt and the excessive vapors with which it fills the vessels and forces them to burst.

Next, take **antimony** reduced to a fine powder, purified saltpeter, and **calcined tartar**, <term>calcined tartar: potassium carbonate produced by heating the sediment from wine barrels</term> three ounces [℥ iij.] of each. Let them be melted in a melting pot, and let the vessel be struck with an iron rod so that the **regulus** <term>regulus: the metallic "heart" or button that settles at the bottom of a crucible after smelting</term> sinks to the bottom. Once the vessel has cooled on its own, let it be broken, and the regulus separated from the **dross** <note>original: "loppa" — the waste material or slag left over from smelting</note>, and ground into a powder. Take one ounce [℥. j.] of this powder and place it in a wide-mouthed glass vessel. Moisten it with the Caesar’s or King’s water and dry it over hot ashes; repeat this moistening and drying six times. Finally, let it be dissolved in the same **aqua regia** <note>original: "aqua regia" — "Royal Water," a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving noble metals</note>, and let the solution be kept separately.

Afterwards, in the same water, also dissolve separately one ounce [℥. j.] of gold, either in thin plates or beaten into leaves. Mix these three solutions together, and through a single vessel, very well sealed with **lute** <term>lute: a clay or paste used to seal chemical apparatus airtight</term> and a retort, [distill it] six times...

Save the oil, which is to be administered in theriacal water in doses ranging from a scruple <term>scruple: an apothecary weight roughly equal to 1.3 grams</term> to a drachm <term>drachm: an apothecary weight roughly equal to 3.9 grams</term>, etc.

### *Preparation of theriacal water.*
**Take:** Spirit of wine <note>alcohol</note>: 5 ounces.
**Theriac:** 2 and a half ounces. <note>Theriac was a famous ancient "cure-all" compound often containing dozens of ingredients including opium and viper flesh.</note>
**Red rose myrrh:** 1 ounce, 2 drachms.
**Oriental saffron:** 2 drachms.
Mix all together and let them be distilled once.

### *Its use in administration.*
Let one and a half drachms of the Arcanum <term>Arcanum: a "secret" or highly purified alchemical essence</term> or tincture of corals be mixed with ten ounces of chicory water, or water of germander or wall-germander. Let a quarter of an ounce of this mixture be given to the patient to drink in the morning and evening—five hours before the midday meal and the same number of hours after dinner; and do this for six or seven continuous days. Let the patient be fed with lighter foods, and for drink use water distilled from fumitory <note>original: "fumo terræ," literally "smoke of the earth"</note>, chicory, or similar plants, which should be used only moderately. When no more purulent <note>pus-filled</note> fluid is seen to flow from the ulcers, one must stop using the tincture, as is done with other treatments.

### *Regarding the Tincture of Balsam.*
**Take:** Balsam: half an ounce.
**Spirit of wine:** 20 ounces.
Let them be circulated together in a pelican <term>pelican: a specialized circulatory distillation vessel with two "arms" that return condensed liquid to the base</term> in the sun for the space of one month. Afterward, let both be distilled through an alembic; this distillation is better than one performed using a retort <note>a retort is a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck</note>. This mixture remains as a single composition, to which finally another half-ounce of balsam should be added; let it be digested <note>original: "digeratur"; a process of gentle, sustained heating</note> in a closed glass vessel as before, and mixed with the other ingredients.

[space] ->*On the administration and use of the tincture of balsam.*<- [space]

Give to the sick person this prepared balsam—the weight of five barleycorns—in old white wine, twice every day, until no more pus flows from the ulcers. Once the origin of the ulcers is removed, the effect ceases through the internal use of this secret remedy <term>arcanum</term> <note>An *arcanum* (plural *arcana*) is a "secret" or "inner" power of a substance that Paracelsian medicine sought to extract to cure the root of a disease rather than just the symptoms.</note>, and they are cured externally.

[space] ->*On the Tincture of Antimony.*<- [space]

Take <note>The original uses the symbol ♃, which traditionally represents the planet Jupiter or the metal Tin, but in this context of a recipe, it likely serves as a shorthand for the command 'Recipe' (Take).</note> of the best Antimony made into a fine powder <term>alcohol</term> <note>original: "in alcool facti"; in early modern chemistry, *alcohol* referred to any substance ground into an extremely fine, impalpable powder, not necessarily a liquid.</note>. You shall urge this with fire in a closed reverberatory vessel <note>A furnace designed to reflect or "reverberate" heat back onto the material being heated.</note> for a month. From this it will become volatile: first white, then lemon-yellow, and finally red, purple, and light. From this "lily" <note>original: "lilli"; an alchemical term for a purified essence or the "flower" of a mineral substance.</note> you should draw out its excellent substance using spirit of wine <note>distilled alcohol</note>, to a depth of ten fingers, in a closed vessel. Separate the color and the spirit together, and keep them mixed. This is the supreme preparation of the lily from Antimony. All the philosophers and physicians who existed before Paracelsus labored in vain searching for this secret remedy <term>arcanum</term> and did not find it.

[space] ->*Its use is:*<- [space]

That into a vat of new wine in the month of September or October, for every twenty measures, a half-ounce of this tincture should be poured, and let it be allowed to digest and be purified together with the wine. Let the sick person drink this wine

206 **ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES**

the patient [should continue the treatment] for three, four, or more weeks; omitting other medicinal drinks, let the ulcers be covered lightly with more common plasters. For the tincture works effectively enough from within to heal the external parts.

->*All the difficulty lies in this word, alcohol.*<-
<note>The author is highlighting a common linguistic confusion in 16th and 17th-century chemistry regarding the term "alcohol."</note>

Some quite inconsiderately interpret this word (**Alcohol**) and rashly assert it to be a powder ground very finely, as if it were made with an iron pestle and mortar, or other hard instruments. However, the error is sufficiently proved by the fact that in various places **Paracelsus** <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), the Swiss physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> writes about the **alcohol of wine** <note>original: "alcoole vini"</note>, understanding by this "burning water" <note>original: "aquam ardentem"; this refers to distilled spirits or ethanol.</note> that has been rectified, having a far different form and being entirely alien to a powder. It is clear, therefore, that the **Alcohol** of any things or bodies is nothing other than their purer and cleaner substance, separated from its own impurity.

Let us see, then, what the **alcohol of antimony** <note>Antimony is a metallic element that was a cornerstone of Paracelsian medicine, though it was often toxic if prepared incorrectly.</note> might be. In this matter, there must be a discreet judgment between solids and liquids. Solid things <note>original: "concreta"</note> can also be reduced into a most minute substance by means other than those mentioned before—namely by fire, heat, solutions, congelations, sublimations, etc. This is the opinion of our author: that antimony should not only be ground into a powder with a pestle and mortar, but also into a certain volatile substance, and one not changed from its native color. This must necessarily be done by the benefit of fire and heat alone, and by their governance, so that after it has been commonly ground, it may at last be **philosophically** ground. <note>In alchemy, "philosophical" preparation refers to using chemical processes like distillation or sublimation to change the state of a matter, rather than simple physical force.</note>

<summary>This page clarifies the definition of "alcohol" in early modern medicine, explaining that it refers to the purest essence of a substance—whether liquid like wine or solid like antimony—refined through heat rather than just physical grinding.</summary>
<keywords>alcohol, Paracelsus, antimony, spirits of wine, alchemy, distillation, pharmacy, ulcers</keywords>

### BOOK ONE. 207

...ground through sublimations, which are the <term>pestles and mortars</term> <note>original: "piſtilla... & mortaria"</note> of the philosophers. He teaches in another place that this sublimation of antimony should be performed without any <term>dead head</term> <note>original: "capite mortuo"; the dross or useless residue left in the vessel after distillation or sublimation</note>. It is quite clear, therefore, that nothing else is present with it.

This sublimation of <term>stibium</term> <note>An older name for the mineral ore of antimony</note> is necessary first; otherwise, the work is undertaken in vain, and a useless labor is spent on the <term>flowers</term> <note>A powdery substance produced by sublimation, named for its light, petal-like appearance</note> and <term>tincture</term> <note>A medicinal extract or essence</note> of antimony. The majority of those who perform the "anatomy" <note>A term for the chemical analysis or breaking down of a substance into its components</note> of Paracelsian preparations are deceived when attempting this secret. Immediately after the common grinding of stibium, they sublimate it into a white soot. But they have more often learned to their own loss that they could sooner climb to heaven than reach the true yellow and red flowers of antimony. This is no common method, but a purely philosophical artifice, known to very few until now. Therefore, it is to be revealed to the "sons of doctrine" <note>A traditional term for dedicated students of the alchemical arts</note>. A sublimation of this kind must be done with a very moderate fire, so that the powder of antimony does not melt, yet ascends nonetheless, just as the **flower of flour** <note>the finest, lightest dust of the grain</note> is seen clinging to the walls of flour mills. Behold, now you have the entire secret of this business, and (unless you are as dull as a wooden stump) you can understand what I mean.

Let the water be drawn off through a <term>sand bath</term> <note>original: "per arenam"; a method of heating a vessel evenly by nesting it in hot sand</note> with a fierce fire, and a liquid in the form of milk will sweat out; when poured back again, it turns into a very white oil, sweet beyond all corrosion, excelling all other oils of metals. Digested and congealed with the **sun** <note>original: "sole"; the alchemical symbol for Gold</note>, it can manifest the highest mastery of the art, or the **elixir**. Administered by mouth, it expels hidden ulcerations in the throat, bladder, and kidneys through sweating and cures them radically.

*Sweet Essence of Mercury.*

Let it be dissolved in water of saltpeter, and when drawn off by distillation, let it be reduced to a white powder. This powder, having been taken up in a melting vessel and placed over glowing coals, should have **aqua fortis** <note>literally "strong water"; a historical name for nitric acid</note>—derived from vitriol and alum—poured over it and then drawn off. Let the powder be washed several times with distilled common water so that it sets aside its sharpness <note>original: "acrimoniam"; the burning, corrosive quality of the chemicals</note>. Afterwards, pour on **burning water**, and draw it off by distillation; repeat this ten times. Finally, with an extremely fierce fire so that the vessel is nearly glowing, draw off almost all the moisture, until the powder of mercury turns red like blood and is perceived to be very sweet to the taste.

# BOOK ONE 209

## A Completing Addition.
Take: 1 pound of the previously prescribed mixture.
Half a pound of washed turpentine <note>Washed turpentine was resin purified by being agitated with water</note>.
Mix these while quite hot, but do not let them boil;
and add Galbanum <note>a gum resin from Persian plants</note> and
Opoponax <note>a medicinal herb resin, often called "sweet myrrh"</note>, 1 ounce of each.
These latter ingredients, however, should first be boiled in vinegar so that they may be more easily incorporated into the mixture. Finally, add 1 and a half ounces of powdered Round Birthwort <term>Aristolochia rotunda: a plant used historically in wound healing, named for its supposed aid in childbirth</term>. Let all these be mixed together. With this ointment, any ulcers that contain no corrosive salt are thoroughly healed.

## *The Treatment of Ulcers using Calcined Materials*
Take: 10 egg yolks.
7 ounces of Turpentine.
Bring these into the form of an ointment, as is the custom; to which you should add and mix in half an ounce of a non-corrosive calcined <note>original: "calcinati" — a substance reduced to powder through intense heat</note> material.
Bind the dressing on in the morning and evening: in a wonderful manner, it heals ulcers by cleansing them.

## *The Closing of Ulcers.*
Take: 1 pound of common resin.
Half a pound of wax.
3 ounces of Greek pitch <note>original: "Picis græcæ" — also known as colophony or rosin</note>.
When all are mixed, stir in 1 ounce of calcined material.

## *The Treatment of Malignant Ulcers using Corrosive Calcined Salts.*
Take: Alum, burnt and extinguished in vinegar. Oil of arsenic made <note>original: "o factum" — likely "oleo factum" or referring to the preparation process</note> through its own resolution <note>the process of a solid dissolving into liquid</note>.

BOOK ONE. 109
*The three bodies of mercury.*

*The First.*
Before it breaks forth from its mineral matrix and the earth; this must be taken up with its own body <note>original: "cum suo corpore"; referring to the raw ore or "mother" material before the metal is extracted</note> and prepared for use in the preparation of mercury.

*The Second.*
After it has been born—that is, when it exists in itself, having been separated from its body by a medium fire.

*The Third.*
When it has been prepared by art and reduced into the form of a "burnt" metal <note>original: "adusti metalli"; likely referring to a calcined or oxidized state of the metal</note>.

*The first preparation of Mercury.*
Take: <note>The OCR symbol ♃, usually representing Jupiter or Tin, is likely used here as a shorthand for "Recipe" or "Take."</note> Of the ore in which mercury is born, as it is found in its first coagulation, not yet hardened nor formed into a body, 20 pounds. Let it be ground and cooked in rain water for one hour. The impurities will separate by themselves and rise to the surface; remove these by tilting the vessel, and let the sediment—namely the "body"—be dried, ground, and passed through a sieve. Afterward, you shall distill it from a cucurbit <term>cucurbita: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</term> using a most vehement fire. A very clear and white liquor will ascend; save this, and separate the phlegm <note>original: "phlegma"; the watery, inactive part of a distillate</note> from it using a water bath <term>balneum maris: a "bath of Mary" or double boiler used for gentle, even heating</term>. In the bottom there will remain a certain heavy oiliness, which is slightly less than the mercury itself. Use this "juice of the immature ore" at a weight of two grains, with six grains of oil of spike <note>original: "olei spicæ"; an essential oil derived from spike lavender, often used as a solvent or topical stimulant</note>. Use this according to mercurial practice.

*The second preparation.*
Take: Bodily mercury <note>mercury in its metallic form</note>, and amalgamate it with an equal weight of tin <note>original: "stanni"; also symbolized by ♃</note>. Let them flow together into water...

| Ingredient | Quantity |

->**Removal of swellings.**<-

St. John's Wort flowers: 2 handfuls of each.
Elderflowers: 3 handfuls.
Chamomile flowers: 2 1/2 handfuls.

Cook all these together in wine and vinegar. Afterwards, once all the liquid has been squeezed out, let them be placed over the injury until the swelling goes away.

Take: An ointment made from egg yolks, which is made more potent in its effect when mixed with calcined material <term>calcinato: a substance reduced to powder through high heat, used here to dry the wound or strip away dead tissue</term>. Fill the wound with this twice every day until you perceive a very healthy wound bed <note>original: "fundum optimum"</note> that is free from the stench of impurities, etc.

*Preparation of Mercury.*
Coagulate mercury using alum water, and reduce the mercury so coagulated into a powder; mix it with brown ointment, and smear it on until you have a fresh base. It affects the patient with no pain. <note>Mercury was a staple of early modern medicine, though highly toxic; practitioners sought ways to "fix" or "coagulate" it to harness its healing properties for skin ailments.</note>

*Composition of a plaster for the cure.*
Take: <term>Litharge <note>original: "Lithargyrij"; lead(II) oxide, historically used in ointments for its drying and healing properties.</note></term>
Oil of wax, 1 pound of each.
Make it boil in that wax-mixture,
then throw in 1 pound of <term>Colophony <note>original: "Colophoniæ"; a distilled pine resin, or rosin, used to make the plaster sticky.</note></term>.
1 ½ ounces of Frankincense, and finally,
2 ½ ounces of Varnish.
Reduce this to the form of a plaster.

Let them be dried with <term>Saffron of Mars <note>original: "croco Martis"; an alchemical term for iron oxide (rust), used as an astringent to dry out wounds.</note></term> and cleansed as described above; that will be sufficient. Let the wound have a vent, whether it flows or not.

*Cure of ulcers arising from their own constellation.*
Take: Celandine, 3 handfuls.
Oak leaves, 2 handfuls.
Let them be ground, let them rot in dung <note>This process, known as "putrefaction," used the natural heat of decomposing manure to break down organic matter in alchemical preparations.</note>, and let them be distilled into an oil; with this you shall wash the area until the flesh grows back, and you shall sprinkle upon the ulcers a powder ground from these same plants. This will be your most certain remedy for "constellated" ulcers. <note>In the medical philosophy of the time, "constellated" diseases were thought to be influenced by specific astrological alignments or possessed an "astral" character that required specific sympathetic cures.</note>

220 **ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.**

->***Definitions and Cures for Complications or Symptoms of Wounds.***<-

->***A Paroxysm in Wounds***<-
<note>original: "Paroxismus"</note>
This is said to occur when wounds first become extremely hot, and afterward a chill sets in, similar to fevers that cycle on alternating days or hours.

->***Burning Heat***<-
<note>original: "Ardor"</note>
Where a heat arises in the wound which spreads through the whole body, then ceases, and returns at its own fixed time without any following chill.

->***Numbness***<-
<note>original: "Stupefactio"</note>
This is said to occur when the wound seems to "fall asleep," as if ants were scurrying about inside it. <note>This sensation is what we now call "pins and needles" or formication.</note>

Take:
*   **Calamine stone**
*   **Litharge** <note>Lead oxide, a traditional ingredient in medicinal plasters.</note>
*   **Red lead** <note>original: "Minij"</note> — **half a pound of each.** <note>The symbol "℔" denotes a pound; "ß" denotes a half.</note>
*   **Tutty** <note>A mineral form of zinc oxide collected from smelting furnaces.</note> — **2 ounces.** <note>The symbol "℥" denotes an ounce.</note>
*   **Wax**
*   **Myrtle oil** — **9 ounces of each.**

Cook these together into a <term>cerate</term> <note>A stiff ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal ingredients.</note> and apply it. Similarly, **camphor oil** smeared upon the wounds is effective. Also, a half-ounce of **Mithridatic conserve** <note>A famous ancient "universal" remedy and antidote consisting of many ingredients.</note> taken as a drink. Each of these cures by itself, or they may be used together as the necessity of the case dictates.

->***Swelling of Wounds, or a "Sack"***<-
<note>The term "sack" likely refers to a localized pocket of fluid or a cyst-like swelling.</note>
This occurs when the wound swells up along with heat, as if...

# BOOK ONE 231

...so that if it is pressed with a finger, it leaves a lingering indentation, as is seen in a "watery hernia" <note>original: "hernia aquosa"; likely referring to edema or a soft, fluid-filled swelling that pits when pressed</note>; but other types of tumors are not merely accidental symptoms.

### Treatment of such tumors.

Take: Watercress,
Water-lily, an equal amount of each as shall seem sufficient.
Boil them together in rose vinegar <note>Vinegar infused with rose petals, frequently used in historical medicine for its supposed cooling and astringent properties</note> and apply it while warm.

### Another treatment.

Apply burnt clay taken from kilns <note>original: "e furnis argillam adustam"; likely terracotta or brick dust used as a drying agent</note> boiled in rose vinegar.

### Another.

Apply melons <note>original: "Melaones"; refers to various gourds or melons used in poultices</note> boiled in similar vinegar, or for those who have suffered a strike <note>original: "Ictis"; referring to those suffering from a blow or bruise</note>, apply a sponge boiled in vinegar.

### Worms.

These are born in a wound through the neglect, or rather the lack of skill, of a surgeon who applies an ineffective medicine. This causes the flesh to rot, leading to the generation of worms <note>Commonly referring to maggots; before the discovery of germ theory, it was widely believed that such organisms were "spontaneously generated" from decaying tissue</note>, and so on.

### Putrefaction.

When a wound rots to such an extent that it no longer responds to treatment.

### Fistulation.

This occurs when a wound is healed far too late or the process is delayed; a discharge is introduced, and from this, a hole <term>Foraminatio: the formation of a fistula or a deep, narrow track in the tissue</term> is formed.

### Foul Odor.

Sometimes, whether the wound is healing or not, it emits a stench. This is a sign of either a poor medicine or a "hot nature" <note>In the Galenic system of medicine, a "hot nature" or temperament was thought to accelerate the decomposition of bodily fluids</note>.

### Treatment...

226 ->**ON CURING DISEASES**<-

## Cure
Take: **Arsenical thread**, <note>original: "Filum arſenicale"; a surgical cord treated with arsenic used to "strangle" or cauterize a growth or tumor.</note> with which you may cut off the fungus. Stop the blood, and thoroughly smear the wound with burning oil. By that oil, warts and fungi will be consumed from their very roots and the branches from which they arise. Once the consumption is complete, you shall treat the pores with a **Mummy plaster**. <note>original: "emplaſtro de Mummia"; in early modern medicine, "Mummy" referred to a substance—often bitumen or prepared organic remains—believed to have potent healing and drying properties.</note> If, however, the blood should flow too abundantly, open a vein behind the fungus and cool it, as has been previously stated regarding the suppression of blood.

## Pustulous Fistula
This is detected when a wound is suddenly closed too much before it has laid a proper foundation <note>i.e., before the wound has healed from the bottom up</note>, and for that reason, it rots.

### Cure
| Take: | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Liquor of amber** <note>original: "Liquorum carabis"</note> | |
| **Asphalt** | |
| **Ammoniacum** | half a dram <note>original: "ʒ.ß."</note> of each. |
| **Mummy cerate** <note>A wax-based ointment.</note> | 3 drams <note>original: "ʒ.ij."</note> |

Mix these together into a plaster and apply it.

## Obstruction
This occurs from a weakness of nature, due to an excessive outflow of blood, because of which it happens that the patient has no benefit of the bowels nor of the bladder. <note>The author suggests that extreme blood loss weakens the body's natural "virtue" or strength, leading to constipation and urinary retention.</note>

### Cure
Let the patient be purged with **Sagapenum** <note>original: "Serapino"; a medicinal gum resin from a Persian plant.</note> or **Ultramarine Mummy**, mixed in the proper weight with **wound potions**, which should be used together at the same time.

## Hemoptysis and the Spitting of Blood
This proceeds from clotted blood which, retained inwardly and not at all purged from the beginning, seeks an **emunctory** <note>original: "emunctorium"; a channel or organ through which the body naturally expels waste.</note> through the mouth.

*Cure for Panaritium or the "Worm."*

A **panaritium** <note>original: "Panaritius"; a painful, deep infection of the fingertip, commonly known today as a whitlow or paronychia.</note> or "worm" arises from the very substance from which nails are born, and it centers itself in the joints, such as the fingers of the hands and the toes of the feet.
The cure is threefold: one is performed through **characters** <note>original: "caracteres"; magical symbols or sigils inscribed on amulets or the skin.</note>, as is customarily done in Carinthia; the second is through **superstition** <note>This refers to folk charms, incantations, or ritualistic practices common in early modern medicine.</note>; the third is with natural medicines, such as a narcotic ointment made from **frog spawn** <note>original: "spermiolo ranarum"</note>, with which a small pouch, sewn in the shape of a finger, should be moistened and worn, and moistened frequently, etc.
*Little Surgery*, Book 3, Chapter 3.

*Cure for Panaritium, Pandalitium, or "the Passion."*

After cooling the area three or four times with frog spawn, you may cure it with pig dung cooked with vinegar.
*Book on Apostemes <note>term: "Aposteme"; an old medical term for an abscess or localized swelling filled with pus.</note>, Chapter 17.*

Cloths <note>original: "Panni"; likely the start of a new section on medical patches or eye films.</note>

<summary>This page concludes instructions on botanical remedies for bone fractures and introduces treatments for "panaritium" (fingertip infections), detailing a mix of magical, superstitious, and natural remedies like frog spawn and vinegar-cooked dung.</summary>
<keywords>bone healing, Aristolochia, Flixweed, Panaritium, whitlow, folk medicine, frog spawn, Carinthia, abscesses, early modern surgery</keywords>

228 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES

...should be mixed with sulfur powder. Let it be distilled and the oil separated from the water to thoroughly heal ulcers.

*The most excellent treatment for ulcers using oil of Mercury:*

Mercury should be calcined <note>A process of heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to powder, often changing its chemical state.</note> using *aqua fortis* <note>original: "aquam fortem," or "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid</note> several times, and afterwards it should be washed with *aqua ardens* <note>original: "aqua ardente," or "burning water," referring to high-proof distilled alcohol</note> so that it sheds the corrosive nature of the nitric acid. Let it be digested in vinegar, and its salt extracted through a filter. The vinegar should be driven off by a water bath <note>original: "balneum," likely a *balneum Mariae* or double boiler</note> so that it turns into a dry white powder. This should be washed with celandine water; finally, once dried, it is left in a humid place to dissolve into a crystal-clear liquid used for healing any kind of ulcer.

*Treatment for ulcers in the mouth.*

Take: Cemented copper <note>Copper purified through a heating process called "cementation"</note>, 2 ounces.
White burnt alum and rock alum, 6 ounces of each.
Distilled vinegar, 1 pound.
Let the alkali be extracted.
*The following can be added:*
To this, "flower of copper" <note>original: "floris æris," likely verdigris or a copper acetate</note>, 1 ounce.
Celandine water, 3 ounces.
Alum water, 1 ounce.
Let a gargle be made from this.
From the *Book of Preparations*, regarding copper.

*The cleansing of ulcers by Cardonus made from vitriol.*

Let 5 ounces of this <note>Cardonus likely refers to a specific crystalline formation or preparation resembling a thistle (Latin: *carduus*)</note> be suspended in twenty measures of fresh wine or grape must <note>original: "musti," unfermented grape juice</note>, and let [the ulcers] be cleansed with it; one spoonful of it should be taken at a time.

->*The cure of ulcers through balsams.*<-

Take 4 scruples of gold leaf <note>original: "Solis foliati." In alchemy, the Sun (Sol) represents Gold</note>.

Finally, collect the balsam by means of a water bath <note>original: "balneum," likely a bain-marie</note>. This cures far greater things than ulcers.

->*Balsam from iron.*<-
First, by means of distilled vinegar, let it be reduced to a saffron-colored powder <note>original: "crocum," a term for a metallic oxide, in this case, iron oxide</note> by reverberation <note>intense heating in a furnace where the flame is "reverberated" or reflected onto the material</note>. From this, finally, let sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note> be distilled nine times, and afterward let it be reduced into a perfectly sweetened oil.

->*The use of both is:*<-

Let the golden balsam be applied to the ulcers three times every week.
But the iron balsam once every twelve hours.

->*Balsam from copper.*<-
Reduce the copper again into its vitriol <note>copper sulfate</note>; then [reduce] that into water, and the *colcothar* <note>the red residue left after the distillation of vitriol</note> into a red oil.

->*Balsam from lead, or the sweetness of Saturn.*<-
Let the lead <note>referred to here by its alchemical name, Saturn</note> be reduced to ashes, from which its spirit may be drawn off through distilled vinegar, so that a sweetness of this kind <note>this refers to "sugar of lead" or lead acetate, which is sweet-tasting but highly toxic</note> may pass into an oil.

Balsam...

### Healing

Afterward, you shall apply wound plasters made of litharge <note>original: "litargyrio"; lead oxide used in ointments</note> and colophony <note>original: "colofonia"; a type of pine resin</note>, and treat the wound with these. Every fifth day you shall renew this plaster.

### Conclusion

Take: Saffron of Mars <note>original: "Crocum Martis"; a term for iron oxide or rust used for its astringent properties</note>, which has been burnt or reverberated <term>reverberated: a process of heating a substance in a furnace where the flame is "reverberated" or reflected back onto the material</term> by itself, without the addition of vinegar or any corrosive substance. Take:
Common water — 1 ounce <note>The symbol ℥ represents an ounce</note>
Alum — 2 ounces
Common salt — 1/2 ounce <note>The symbol ß represents a half</note>

Having mixed all these, you shall wash the wound, and once the ulcer is dried, you shall conclude the treatment by sprinkling the "saffron" <note>the iron oxide powder mentioned above</note> over it.

### The treatment of ulcers gathering together

Take:
Ointment of egg yolks — 3 ounces
Oil of Mercury — 1 ounce

With these mixed together, bind the wound until the healing is complete; or, in place of the Oil of Mercury, take 2 ounces of the "Great Calcined." Or in place of all these, take a wound ointment mixed with the "Great Calcined," according to the extent of the damage, etc.

FIRST BOOK. 246

...extract it using rainwater, which you shall then coagulate; from that same coagulated material, extract the redness once again. Do this five or six times, or even more, until the matter solidifies into a hardness like a stone, which, when touched to the tongue, yields a supreme sweetness. By this remedy, *syrones* <note>original: "syrones"; likely referring to scabies mites or similar skin parasites</note> and other most serious ulcers are cured. However, the greatest sweetness is found in the vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate of a metal, in this case copper, often used in early medicine as an astringent</term> which is chemically extracted from copper.

*The Sweetness of Antimony, or its Balsam or Mummy.*

Dissolve antimony with sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to help dissolve metals</term>, then finally sweeten it <note>original: "dulcorato"; in this context, it means to wash away the salts with water until neutral</note> with rainwater. Afterward, once it is dried, dissolve it upon a marble slab as many times as necessary until it possesses a very deep red, clear, and transparent color. With this medicine, fistulas and cancerous ulcers—even those complicated by the venereal disease <note>a reference to syphilis</note>—are cured most effectively.

*The Balsam of Quicksilver, its Sweetness, and its Blood-like Power.*

Let the "water of eggs" <note>likely a distillate of egg whites or a preparation involving eggshells</note> distilled over its own lime (in which Mercury has been extinguished) be reduced into a very deep red powder. Prepared in this manner, it acquires a wonderful sweetness by which ulcers and wounds—even those of the bladder which are otherwise incurable, as well as throat ailments resulting from common excessive gluttony—are cured most excellently, perfectly, and completely from the very root.

*The Balsam, Sweetness, or Mummy of Arsenic and Orpiment.*

These must be fixed <term>fixed: a process of making a volatile substance solid or resistant to heat</term> by means of saltpeter, and once fixed, they must be dissolved. Once dissolved, they are to be sublimated in an alembic <term>alembic: a distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</term>, and turned into a fatty water...

-> *Balsam of Copper* <-

This vitriol is drawn from copper—that is, from its "flowers"—through dissolution and then coagulation in alkali. It is most beneficial for the treatment of corrosive ulcers.

->**A miraculous, magnetic cure for wounds.**<-

Take the herb <term>Persicaria</term> <note>Also known as water-pepper or smartweed (Persicaria hydropiper), known for its acrid taste and red-stained stems.</note>—otherwise called "pepper" or "water-blood"—as much as you wish, washed in fresh water or simply moistened. # BOOK ONE. 159

### The Cure for Scabies and Venereal Itching

Take:
10 pounds of Alum.
1 pound of Vitriol <note>original: "Vitrioli"; a historical name for various metal sulfates, often used for their astringent properties.</note>.
Half a pound of Calcined Tartar <note>original: "Tartari calcinati"; potassium carbonate produced by burning crude tartar, used as an alkaline agent.</note>.
3 pounds of Fused Salt.

Let all these be cast into water in which **hops** <note>original: "lupulus"</note> have previously been soaked; in this bath, the sick may be washed. If the disease is long-standing, extinguish **glowing mercury** <note>mercury heated until it vaporizes or glows</note> four times in a fourth part of dissolved vitriol, and use it as an ointment. This material may be thickened in the following manner:

Take:
1 ounce each of Calcined Alum and <term>Plumose Alum<note>original: "aluminis plumosi"; a fibrous form of alum or asbestos-like mineral used in ancient medicine for its drying effects.</note></term>.
Calcined Tartar.
4 ounces each of <term>Colcotar Vitriol<note>original: "Vitrioli colchotarini"; the red iron oxide residue left after the distillation of sulfuric acid from vitriol.</note></term>.
Rock Alum.
Black Salt.
1 pound each of Raw Vitriol.
A quantity of **cheese prepared into a gluey mass** <note>original: "Casei præparati in viscum"; casein from cheese was often used as a binding agent or adhesive in early medicinal pastes.</note> equal to the weight of all the other ingredients.

Let all these, once mixed, be reduced to a paste, which becomes as hard as **horn** in the air; and when you wish to use it, cast it into hot water, and it will dissolve.

A salt made from **feverfew** <note>original: "parthenio"</note>, **serpentine** <note>original: "Serpentina"; likely referring to bistort or another plant used to treat snakebites and skin sores.</note>, and **briony** <note>original: "Prionia"</note>, when added to the prescribed water, cures scabies and the itching born from the <term>venereal disease<note>In the 16th century, this usually referred to the "Great Pox" or syphilis, which caused significant skin lesions.</note></term>.

Likewise, a <term>phlegm<note>original: "phlegma"; in early chemistry, this referred to the watery distillate or "spirit" pulled from a substance through heat.</note></term> distilled from saltpeter, when mixed with thermal water, far surpasses the power of Mercury in the treatment of those suffering from <term>dropsy<note>original: "hydropicorum"; an old term for edema or swelling caused by fluid retention.</note></term> and ulcers arising from the venereal disease. It is equally effective for **herpetic** <note>original: "herpeticis"; spreading or creeping skin eruptions.</note> conditions.

<summary>This page provides recipes for medicinal baths and hardened pastes intended to treat scabies and skin irritations caused by venereal disease. It highlights the use of mineral salts like alum and vitriol alongside botanical extracts to create shelf-stable treatments.</summary>

### From the Great Surgery, Part 1, Book 2, Chapter 7.

*Cures for wounds by distilled and sublimated substances.*

*Cure of wounds by oil of Antimony.*
Take: Three pounds each of Antimony and calcined <term>rock salt</term> <note>original: "salis gemmae"; a high-purity salt often used in chemical operations</note>. Distill them together through a <term>retort</term> <note>a glass vessel with a downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> and a very strong <term>Athanar</term> <note>original: "Athanar"; also known as an Athanor, this was a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant heat for days</note>. After three natural days, you will have a very red oil, the powers and capabilities of which can hardly be expressed for treating desperate wounds.

*Cure of wounds by sublimated copper.*
Take: The finest copper, and first burn or <term>calcine</term> <note>to heat a substance until it oxidizes and turns to powder</note> it with sulfur. Afterward, dissolve this <term>calx</term> <note>the powdery residue</note> using <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "aquam fortem"; literally "strong water," a term for nitric acid</note>, then let it congeal. Add to this congealed powder twice its weight of liquefied salt. Afterward, <term>sublimate</term> <note>the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again on a cooler surface, used for purification</note> it through a <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>a furnace that reflects or "reverberates" heat from the roof onto the material being heated</note> into a light, green powder. When sprinkled upon wounds, it cures them most quickly and perfectly.

->*Explanation of the chemical requirements for this section.*<-

Distillation, of which mention was made in the preceding sections, is not unlike that which is performed for <term>oil of vitriol</term> <note>sulfuric acid</note> in an Athanar—that is, in a furnace covered at the top, with certain air-holes or vents open at the sides. First, beneath a glass retort vessel protected on all sides by very compact clay or <term>lute of wisdom</term> <note>original: "lutum sapientiae"; a specialized paste used to seal laboratory apparatus airtight and protect it from heat</note>, a light fire should be kindled in the manner of the first stage...

->## 262 ON CURING DISEASES<-

...of the first degree, specifically: after some time, or a quarter of the total duration, the heat must be increased to the second degree. Again, at the midpoint of the duration, the heat is to be strengthened to the third degree, so that the burning coals <note>original: "prunæ"</note> touch the **retort vessel** <term>retort<gloss>a glass or ceramic vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</gloss></term> and rise up to nearly the middle part of the said vessel.

When three-quarters of the distillation time have passed, one must reach the fourth degree of **reverberatory fire** <term>reverberatory fire<gloss>a high-heat method where the furnace roof reflects or "reverberates" the flame back onto the material</gloss></term>; that is, so that the coals completely surround the vessel at the top and the sides. This kind of fire must be continued until the receiving vessel containing the distilled liquid is no longer colored by the <term>spirits<gloss>the volatile, gaseous components of a substance released during heating</gloss></term> of the matter. Having observed this, and being certain that no spirits remain in the retort vessel, let everything cool down once finished, and preserve that which is contained in the receiver vessel.

It should be noted, however, that the heat regimen just described is intended to shorten the time. Furthermore, it is tedious for a person to be strictly bound to a single distillation for three natural days <note>a "natural day" refers to a full 24-hour cycle of day and night</note>, day and night, even if they have a partner to provide relief in turns. I have found through experience that in the case of **oil of vitriol** <term>oil of vitriol<gloss>concentrated sulfuric acid, highly valued by alchemists for its corrosive and transformative properties</gloss></term>, following the process I have provided, the entire business can be completed in eight hours—and more accurately than by sweating over it for four days or more with their continuous nights (as many have written, and even <insert>the famous physician</insert> **Paracelsus** himself). Unless the spirits are driven from the mineral mediums in eight hours by the **graduated fire** which we have taught, they will not be driven out even in the same number of days and nights by any fire, however urgent, whether it be a flaming fire or a melting heat. There are, however, other reverberatory furnaces built for **calcination** <term>calcination<gloss>heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder</gloss></term> and not for distillation.

->*An external remedy for wounds.*<-

Take **Sameth** <note>A term often used in Paracelsian alchemy to refer to a specific salt or prepared tartar.</note> that has been thoroughly burned and calcined to whiteness, to which you should add a smaller amount of **circulatum** <note>A high-quality alchemical solvent or spirit that has been repeatedly distilled.</note>. Distill it afterward, so that a very dry **dead head** <note>original: "caput mortuum," the useless solid residue left after distillation.</note> remains at the bottom, and so that the glass glows with a vehement fire. Then pour on fresh liquid and do as before; repeat this until the *circulatum* comes away from it entirely "sweet" <note>Meaning the solvent has lost its sharpness or has fully extracted the properties of the salt.</note>, just as it is in itself. Finally, let it be allowed to **resolve by itself** <note>To naturally dissolve into a liquid, often by attracting moisture from the air.</note>, and the resulting liquid will be a remedy for every wound, better than all others.

### BOOK ONE. 267

One pound of Greater Comfrey <note>original: "Consolidæ maioris"; a common herb used historically to treat inflammation and heal bones</note>.
One and a half pounds of Adder’s-tongue <note>original: "Ophioglossi"; a type of fern named for the shape of its spore-bearing stalk</note>.
Half a pound of cleansed earthworms.
Three ounces <note>original: "℥.iij."</note> of green Birthwort <note>original: "Aristolochiæ viridis"</note>.

All these should be crushed into a pulp and kept in a cold place; if any moisture separates from the mixture, it should be discarded. This has been the practice of the ancients until now; but hear now what the method of Paracelsus is.

### *The Third Ointment*
### *for Wounds.*

Take the roots and herbs which you prefer to choose from the preceding and following lists, while they are still green, and crush them into a pulp. To these, add a little of the best wine until they are covered. Cook this in a closed jar using a double vessel—a water bath <term>Maris balneum: literally "The Bath of Mary," a vessel placed in boiling water to ensure gentle, even heating</term>—for ten hours. After everything has cooled, take the ingredients out and crush them together, straining as much as possible through a linen cloth or a hair-sieve <term>setaceum: a strainer made from animal bristles</term>.

Take this mixture along with a raw honeycomb (with the honey not yet separated), or with butter alone, or with both mixed together. These should be cooked again in a closed jar; while the mixture is still hot, pass it through a cloth or sieve, or squeeze it through a press. This juice should then be placed in a beaked distilling vessel <term>alembico rostrato: a glass apparatus with a "beak" or spout used for distillation</term> and set in the sun so that it may be purged of its "phlegm" <note>In early chemistry, "phlegm" refers to the watery, tasteless, and medically inactive part of a substance that needed to be removed to concentrate the "spirit" or essence</note>. Afterward, place it in a closed glass bottle and expose it to the sun again; it is then to be preserved as an ointment.

*Washes for deep wounds.*

Take: honey, wine, alum, salt, etc., as much as seems appropriate. In the meantime, wound-healing drinks and plasters should be used.

*Remedy for the poisonous bites of dogs.* <note>In this context, "poisonous" or "venomous" bites from dogs almost always refers to rabies.</note>

Give the patient cold water, or beer, to drink—and in such great abundance that it induces extreme nausea and vomiting. And let the food be very cold—
