_Cure for Viper Bites_

Let the patient be given oil to drink in which glowing steel has been repeatedly quenched. To a pound of this oil, add 2 ounces of red coral powder and half an ounce of 'gem-based' spices <note>original: 'specierum de gemmis'—likely a cordial powder containing ground precious stones, believed to have heart-strengthening properties</note>. The first drink should be hot, but the others cold, administered five times each day. Afterward, the patient's drink should be wine mixed with the best theriac <term>a famous, complex polypharmaceutical 'universal' antidote containing many ingredients</term>, and with every draught, half an ounce of red coral. These potions should be continued until an evident sign of recovery appears. Where excessive thirst overwhelms the patient, warm milk with coral powder should be administered. If death is near, let *ladanum* <note>original: 'ladanum'—here likely referring to an opiate-based 'secret' remedy or 'arcanum' rather than the rockrose resin</note> from our secrets be administered, as will be taught below in its proper place. *Philonium Persicum* <term>an ancient opiate preparation named after Philo of Tarsus, used for pain and cooling the 'blood'</term> is also excellent when mixed into such drinks.

<note>original: "Vulnerum sticticum fodicatorum"; "sticticum" refers to wounds made by a point or sting, while "fodicatorum" refers to those that have been "dug" or pierced deeply.</note>

Both types of coral <note>Referring to red and white coral, which were ground into fine powders and believed to have cooling and strengthening properties for the flesh.</note>.
Magnet <note>Lodestone.</note>.

# 282 ON CURING DISEASES

...one <note>The specific unit of liquid is likely mentioned on the previous page</note> in which you should put one <term>handful</term> <note>original: M. j., meaning manipulus, a standard apothecary measure of a bunch of herbs held in the hand</note> of the same **wintergreen herb**, **sanicle**, and **periwinkle**, <term>half a handful of each</term> <note>original: ana M.ß.</note>. Cook these together according to the previous process in a well-closed vessel so that it does not vent <note>original: ne respiret; literally "so it does not breathe," preventing the escape of steam or medicinal vapors</note>. This will be explained more fully below.

## Fifth Wound Potion

Take 2 pounds of **juniper berries**, very well ground, along with the following:
**Both types of wintergreen**.
**Honeysuckle** <note>original: Periclimeni</note>.
**Roots of white sanicle**, half a handful of each.
**Adder’s tongue**, one and a half handfuls.
**Greater comfrey**.
**Birthwort** or **Comfrey**, five ounces of each.
**Smartweed**, four ounces.

Let these be prepared according to the art of distillation. In the same water resulting from this distillation, wound-healing herbs may be cooked as desired, as the physician deems appropriate, with juniper berries added again, along with cinnamon.

## Sixth Wound Potion

Take one handful of **Lavender flowers** <note>original: Florum Spicæ</note>.
Four handfuls of **St. John’s Wort** <note>original: Hypericonis, or Perforatæ, referring to the "perforated" appearance of the leaves</note>.
Two handfuls of **Mullein**.
**Betony**.
**Lesser centaury**.
**Self-heal**... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "Prunel-" which refers to Prunella vulgaris</note>

*The treatment for soft swellings.* <note>original: "Vndimiæ cura." In early modern medicine, "Undimia" or "Ondimia" typically refers to an edematous swelling or a soft, fluid-filled tumor.</note>

Take:
Greater Comfrey, 2 ounces.
Birthwort, 3 ounces.
Sweet Flag <note>Calami aromatici</note>, 1 ounce.
Orris root <note>Radicum Ireos</note>, 4 ounces.
Periwinkle, 5 handfuls.
White Sanicle, half a handful.
Peacock tail feathers, using their tips for probes <term>Specilla: small medical tools used to explore or clean wounds and apply medicine.</term>, 2 scruples.
Myrrh.
Mastic.
Frankincense.
Mummy <term>Mummy: a substance made from bitumen or resin-soaked remains, highly valued in old pharmacy for its supposed ability to stop bleeding and dry out wounds.</term>, of each half an ounce.
Rhubarb, 5 ounces.
Let them be boiled in wine, or in distilled waters as mentioned above, or in the juice of Wintergreen <note>Pyrola</note>, or in common water, as shall seem most convenient.

*Explanation.*

It is necessary to explain to those who are unfamiliar with the art of distillation what the author means in this passage by a "closed vessel" <note>original: "cantharum clausum"</note>.
A pewter jar should be used, with its lid very well fitted. The crushed wound-healing herbs should be placed and stored inside it with wine. Immediately, the seam of the lid must be secured with a paste made of white wheat flour and beaten egg whites, so that when it is dried... <note>This process of sealing a vessel to prevent the escape of vapors is known as "luting."</note>

<meta>This page continues a technical description of a pharmaceutical distillation process, likely following a list of ingredients or a specific recipe for a medicinal extract.</meta>

->284 ON CURING DISEASES<-

...dried by themselves in mashes covered with a linen cloth, so that not even a tiny bit of the spirits of wine <note>original: *vini spiritu*; high-proof alcohol used as a solvent to extract medicinal properties</note> can evaporate. Once this is done, take a copper cauldron more than half-filled with water. Place the aforementioned jar inside it so that it sits on the bottom of the cauldron, with its top protruding out of the water by at least a span and a half <note>a *sesquipalmo* is roughly 12 inches or 30 cm</note>. Afterwards, light a fire beneath the cauldron, doing so gradually so that the water boils slightly. 

By this artifice, the powers of the herbs and the wine will be blended together without any loss. Due to the ignorance of both ancient and modern practitioners, decoctions <note>herbal extracts made by boiling</note> made in open vessels are often ineffective; all the active properties of the ingredients evaporate, leaving behind nothing but a dreg-filled, tasteless, and empty residue that is more harmful to the sick than beneficial or useful. 

The distillation of juniper berries, which was mentioned earlier, should be understood in this same way. After the berries have been crushed together with the herbs, they are placed in a copper vessel that has been tinned on the inside (similar to the vessels used by those who work at distilling "burning waters" <note>original: *ardentibus aquis*; refers to distilled spirits or brandy</note> from wine). 

Once all the joints have been perfectly sealed, a long pipe should be fitted to the beak of the vessel’s "helmet" <note>original: *galeae*; the cap or "alembic" head of the distilling apparatus</note>. This pipe should pass through a large enough wooden vat filled with cold water—this is called a <term>refrigeratorium</term> <note>a cooling condenser used to turn medicinal vapors back into liquid</note>. At the end of the pipe protruding from the cooling vat, a glass vessel should be attached to catch the liquid as it flows out. In that receiver, one can see the "phlegm" <note>the watery part of the distillate</note> with the oil floating on the surface. Use these according to the true order of medicine. 

In order that readers may gain a clearer understanding of preparations of this kind, it is worthwhile...

<summary>This page describes the technical process of "closed" distillation and decoction, arguing that open-vessel boiling wastes medicinal properties. It provides specific instructions for distilling juniper berries using a water-cooled condenser setup.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, decoction, juniper berries, spirits of wine, pharmacy, laboratory equipment, condenser, medicinal oils</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a technical discourse on pharmaceutical chemistry, specifically the "spagyric" or alchemical preparation of medicines, moving from general surgical theory into the practical handling of substances.</meta>

BOOK ONE. 285

It will be worthwhile to include here some points regarding simple bodies and mixed medicines composed of "simples" <note>In historical medicine, a "simple" is a single medicinal ingredient, usually a specific herb, mineral, or animal product</note>, which no true Surgeon <term>Chirurgus</term> ought to be ignorant of.

Every natural body of a simple substance is twofold: it consists of the pure and the impure. The assembly of more learned physicists has determined that the impure part should be discarded through the process of separation. The pure part, however, is reserved to serve as a vehicle or a "tether"—if I may call it that—for the soul <note>The active essence or vital principle of the medicine</note> of each simple. 

The learned school of spagyrists <note>Early chemists who practiced the "spagyric" art of separating and then recombining the essential components of plants and minerals</note> calls this kind of separation "preparation." They place the adaptation of these simples under a fourfold mastery <note>original: *magisterio*; referring to specific chemical procedures or stages of refinement</note>. 

Some simples do not require the loss of either "body" <note>referring to their pure or impure components</note>, but are instead prepared using both. Certain others require being steeped and mixed into a foreign substance <note>An "alien" body or medium</note>, in which they are far more active than when left on their own. Indeed, others do not work at all if they are not removed from their natural state or if they remain unchanged within it; yet, once they have been purified, they become highly effective. Still others require being separated from their secondary body through the process of "corruption" <note>original: *corruptionem*; here referring to chemical breakdown, fermentation, or putrefaction used to release the inner essence</note>...

<summary>This page introduces the chemical principles of "simples," arguing that a surgeon must understand how to separate the pure, active "soul" of a medicine from its impure physical dross through alchemical preparation.</summary>
<keywords>spagyric medicine, simples, pharmaceutical preparation, alchemy, purification, medicinal essence, surgery</keywords>

*Book of Abscesses, chapter 56.*

*A most powerful ointment for a wicked sore, "Touch-Me-Not," or a rust-colored ulcer.*
<note>"Touch-me-not" (original: *Noli me tangere*) was a historical term for a corrosive or malignant skin ulcer, such as skin cancer, so named because it was easily irritated by touch.</note>

Take: Sublimated realgar <term>Realgar</term> <note>Realgar is a naturally occurring arsenic sulfide mineral; in early medicine, it was used for its caustic properties to "eat away" diseased flesh.</note> 1 ounce.

Let them be cooked in vinegar for two hours, and when it has dried, you shall add 3 ounces of marjoram oil. By mixing, create an ointment, which you will bind to the ulcers very thinly spread on cloths quite often, and you shall grease the surrounding parts with oil so they do not become dry. Let it remain thus for two days and their nights. Finally, it will begin to eject discharge <term>sanies</term> <note>A thin, greenish or bloody fluid discharged from a wound or ulcer</note> and water. Once this is noticed, place the following cleansing plaster over it, and let it remain for as long as it takes until no discharge or any fluid flows out.

*A cleansing plaster.*

| | |
| :--- | :--- |

| Resin <note>original: *Visci*; while this can mean mistletoe, in a resinous context it often refers to the "birdlime" or sticky gum of the tree</note> of pine and fir | |
| Larch resin | 1/2 pound of each |
| Larch pitch | 3 ounces |

Let them be melted...

194 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.

...of pure sugar. Let them be mixed. Among the ancients, the dose was half a dram <term>dram: A unit of weight, approximately 3.9 grams; the original symbol is ʒ.ß.</term> every morning until the seventieth year. After that, up to eighty or the end of life, they took one full dram <note>original: ʒ. integram</note> every sixth day. However, they began using this medicine from the fiftieth year onwards, throughout the full count of 365 days in a year.

From the little book on the preparation of Hellebore, <note>Hellebore was a potent plant used in ancient and early modern medicine, particularly for mental disorders and purging humors.</note>

->*Oil of Glass or of Gems.*<-

Let them be ground into a powder, which should be calcined <term>calcination: A process of heating a substance to high temperatures, often with another material like sulfur, to reduce it to a friable state or to oxidize it.</term> with twice as much sulfur, and finally reverberated <term>reverberation: A method of heating where the flame is reflected or "reverberated" off the roof of a furnace onto the material.</term> with a strong fire. Afterward, pour on distilled nettle water, and let them be digested together in horse dung <note>Horse dung was used as a natural, steady heat source in alchemical digestion to maintain a gentle, constant temperature.</note>; then let them be distilled, and again pour fresh nettle water over the dregs <note>original: "feces"; the solid remains after distillation.</note>, and separate by distillation; repeat this until nothing remains at the bottom. Into these distilled waters, cast a small amount of fused salt; by this, the oil will be separated from the water. Then pour distilled water onto the oil and draw it off repeatedly until all sharpness <note>original: "acrimonia"; refers to the caustic or stinging quality of the chemical.</note> departs from the oil.

->*Or:*<-

Gems calcined by sulfur are dissolved in *aqua regia* <note>original: "aqua regia"; literally "royal water," a powerful mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving noble metals like gold.</note>, which is then drawn off by distillation. Afterward, they are washed until sweet with distilled celestial water <note>original: "cœleſti aqua"; likely a term for highly purified water or a specific alchemical distillate.</note>. Finally, by adding spirit of wine <note>High-proof alcohol/ethanol.</note> or vinegar, they are brought into a clear oil.

->*The form of spirit of vitriol.*<-
<note>Spirit of vitriol is an early name for sulfuric acid.</note>

### BOOK ONE. 81

Myrrh. Frankincense, <term>of each</term> <note>original: "ana"</note> 1 <term>dram</term> <note>original symbol: "ʒ"</note>.
Let them be purified by <term>digesting</term> <note>the process of soaking a substance in a liquid with gentle heat to extract its properties</note> in the sun within a glass vessel.
Place cloths soaked in this liquid upon the aching part, and when they have dried, let them be applied again after being re-soaked; continue this until all pain is eased.

## Subsequent treatment.

**Take:** <note>original symbol: "℞"</note> Hardened turpentine, 2 pounds <note>original symbol: "℔"</note>.
<term>Opopanax</term> <note>a yellow-gold gum resin derived from the Hercules-all-heal plant</note>, half a pound <note>original symbol: "℔. ß."</note>.
<term>Colcothar</term> <note>red iron oxide, often used as a drying agent or caustic</note>, 3 drams.
Let these be reduced into a plaster: to which may be added hot and thinned clarified honey <note>original: "mel despumatum," honey with the scum removed by boiling</note>, so that cloths can be dipped into that liquid and applied to the <term>cut limb</term> <note>likely referring to a surgical incision or the site of an amputation</note>.

**You shall harden the turpentine in this way:**
Cook it in a pot over a slow fire; by dipping a small stick in and drawing it out, see if it has hardened; if it does, remove it from the fire. It will take on the appearance of glass. Let it be ground into a powder, and let this powder, together with the opopanax, be slowly cooked in two measures of vinegar until it dissolves. Afterward, let it be strained through a cloth and cooked until the vinegar evaporates and the mixture thickens; finally, let the colcothar and turpentine be added. While the material is still hot, let it be formed into oblong masses, for it hardens like glass when cold. When you wish to use it, melt it by placing it in clarified honey and heating it gently; let this be bound upon the cut limb.

BOOK ONE. 83

Strong water <note>original: "aqua fortis"; a historical term for nitric acid</note> is poured on, and they are left together for as long as it takes for small stones to appear, as clear as crystal. Next, after the moisture has been drawn off through sand, distilled vinegar is gradually poured on, and everything is digested in a bath <note>original: "balneo"; a water bath or "bain-marie" used for gentle, even heating</note> for ten or twelve hours until dissolved. Once the vessel has cooled, the clear liquid is removed and distilled. To this dried material, more distilled vinegar is poured, and it is cooked as before until all the material is dissolved. Once this is accomplished, the vinegar is drawn off by distillation; it is poured on and drawn off until the material remains at the bottom in the form of an oil. This must be removed while it is still hot, lest it thicken into the consistency of honey. It is used like oil of lead to heal any fresh wounds and ulcers within 24 hours.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical preparations, moving from the general theory of crystals to specific metallic and herbal remedies. It establishes a link between gemstone processing and the creation of "Theriacs" or universal medicines.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

Following the process now established for crystals in three preparations—namely **calcination**, **sublimation**, and **distillation**—these transparent gems can be prepared, each according to its specific effects: Garnets, Sapphires, Emeralds, Hyacinths, and Rubies, with their additions as described below in the *Book of Preparations, concerning gems.* <note>The author is applying the three primary alchemical methods of purification—burning to ash (calcination), vaporizing (sublimation), and condensing (distillation)—to precious stones to unlock their medicinal or "virtuous" properties.</note>

*Ladanum of Mercury* <note>original: "Ladanum mercury." In this context, it likely refers to a purified, resin-like extract of mercury rather than the plant resin known as labdanum.</note>
Let the mercury be thoroughly purified in the best possible way and sublimated with equal weights of vitriol <term>vitriol: a sulfate mineral, often used as a corrosive or cleaning agent</term> and saltpeter <term>saltpeter: potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent</term>, having first been worked in a mortar with a wooden pestle using the strongest vinegar—the more often this is done, the better. Afterward, let it be sublimated again from calcined alum and saltpeter; then, finally, it will be thoroughly cleansed. Drench its powder with "burning water" <term>aqua ardente: a high-proof distilled spirit or alcohol</term>, and draw it off; pour more on and draw it off again, until it remains at the bottom in the form of a very white oil. Let it be dried, crushed into a powder, and dissolved upon a marble slab in a damp place. Finally, let it be dried and kept for use. It is called the **Theriac of Metals** <note>A "Theriac" was traditionally a famous, complex herbal jam used as a universal antidote. Here, the author claims this mercury preparation is the metallic equivalent: a supreme healer.</note>. Its dose is one grain <note>roughly 0.05 grams</note> taken with theriac or mithridatium <note>another ancient poly-pharmaceutical cure-all</note> and a most excellent wine.

*Oil of Litharge.*

220 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.
The *Great Surgery*, Part II, Book III, Treatise II, Chapters 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12.

### *Definitions and Treatment of Accidental Symptoms or Complications of Wounds.*

#### *Paroxysm in Wounds*
This is said to occur when wounds first become intensely hot, and afterwards a chill sets in, much like a fever, occurring on alternating days or hours.

#### *Burning*
Where heat arises in the wound which extends through the whole body, then ceases, and returns at its own determined time, with no chill following it.

#### *Numbness*
<note>original: Stupefactio</note>
This is said to happen when the wound seems to "fall asleep," as if ants were scurrying about inside it. <note>This sensation is known in medicine as formication.</note>

### *Treatment of these complications*
♃ <note>The symbol ♃ (Jupiter) is used here as the "Recipe" or "Take" command.</note> Calamine stone. <note>A mineral ore of zinc, used for skin irritations.</note>
Litharge. <note>Lead(II) oxide.</note>
Minium <note>Red lead.</note>, of each half a pound. <note>The symbol ℔ represents a pound; ß represents a half.</note>
Tutty <note>A crude form of zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note>, 2 ounces.
Wax.
Myrtle oil, of each 9 ounces.
Cooked together into a wax-salve <term>ceratum: a stiff ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal ingredients</term>, let these be applied. Similarly, oil of camphor may be smeared on the wounds. Also, half an ounce of Mithridatic conserve <note>Mithridate was a famous "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, believed to cure any poison or exhaustion.</note> taken as a drink; these things cure individually on their own, or may be applied together as necessity requires.

#### *Swelling of Wounds, or "The Bag"*
<note>original: Saccus</note>
This occurs when the wound swells up along with heat, as if...

### The cure for lientery by means of cachimy. ###
<term>Lientery</term><note>original: "Lyenteriæ"; a form of diarrhea where food is passed through the body in an undigested state.</note>
**Take:** <term>Cachimy</term><note>original: "Cachimiam"; a term used by alchemists like Paracelsus to describe the "scum" or dross of metallic ores, often associated with bismuth or antimony.</note> prepared for diarrhea.
**Gum arabic** dissolved in **plantain water**.
Reduce by a "second degree" of fire <note>In alchemy, the second degree of heat is a moderate heat, stronger than a luke-warm bath but not yet boiling.</note> to form a bolus or paste, as described below.
**Let there be added:**
**Of this preparation:** 1 ounce.
<term>Saffron of Mars</term><note>original: "Croci martis"; an alchemical name for iron oxide (rust), used for its astringent and drying properties.</note>: 2 drams.
**Red corals**: 1/2 dram.
<term>Theriac</term><note>original: "Teriacæ"; a famous, complex medicinal electuary used since antiquity as a universal antidote and tonic.</note>: as much as is sufficient to form a bolus.
**The dose** is from 2 drams to 3 or 4, morning and evening every day.
From the *Book on the Preparations of Cachimy*.

[ ❦ ❦ ❦ ❦ ❦ ❦ ❦ ]

### The method for removing spots from the face and the entire skin of the body. ###
An animal, shrub, or fruit—one similar in every way to the shape or likeness that the spot itself represents—should be bound over the spot while it is still alive or fresh. It should be left there until it dies, dries out, or rots of its own accord. By this method, you will have extracted the spot from the flesh only, but not yet from the skin; unless you first moisten the spot on the skin with <term>aqua fortis</term><note>Literally "strong water"; nitric acid, a powerful corrosive.</note> or <term>aqua regia</term><note>Literally "royal water"; a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids.</note>. Then, finally, the skin will turn black for fifteen days or more and will be removed, and another will be reborn in its place, similar to the natural skin.
From the *Book on Worms*, chapter 12.

### Mastery of Pearls <note>original: "Magisterium vnionum." In alchemy, a "Mastery" is a preparation that captures the essential medicinal or transformative power of a substance.</note>

*Magistery from Growing Things.*

Take wood, chopped very minutely; place this in a glazed earthenware pot that is stable in fire. Once covered, let it be burned by a charcoal fire for four hours. Once removed, let it <term>putrefy</term> <note>An alchemical process of slow fermentation or breaking down a substance in a sealed container.</note> in glass for a month, then let it be forced through ashes <note>The use of a sand or ash bath to provide even, indirect heat during distillation.</note> until the very last spirits are produced. Once these are captured, stop immediately, lest the magistery take on a foul smell from the fire. In the same way, seeds, roots, barks, and similar things are brought to their own magistery.

*Magistery from Wine.*

Take the most excellent and best wine, most vigorous in color and flavor, as much as you wish. Pour it into a glass vessel so that it fills only a third part, and once closed with the <term>Seal of Hermes</term> <note>An airtight, "hermetic" seal created by melting the glass neck of a flask shut.</note>, let it be kept in a <term>horse's belly</term> <note>A common alchemical heat source; a pile of fermenting horse manure provided a constant, gentle warmth (roughly 100°F/38°C).</note> for four continuous months of heat that never fails. When this is finished in winter, when the cold and frost are most severe, let it be exposed to them for a month so that it may be frozen

*Method for making*
*juice of pearls.*

Take 1 pound of distilled vinegar.

The material of the pearls will settle at the bottom like a fatty liquor; once this appears, the other liquids (the vinegar and the *aqua vitae*) should be poured off and the juice reserved. Of this, six grains should be given at a time. There are other ways of preparing such a juice, but this is the most useful of all.

*Oil of Mars* <note>In alchemy, Mars is the symbol for iron and steel</note>

Iron filings or steel are reduced into a very red oil by means of alum water, or they are turned into "saffron" <note>original: "crocum"; here referring to a fine reddish powder or iron oxide</note> through distilled vinegar, from which sal ammoniac <note>Ammonium chloride, used as a flux in alchemy</note> is sublimated nine or ten times. Afterwards, it is resolved into an oil, which is washed several times with distilled water; this is more for alchemy than for medical use.

Take: Gold extinguished in celandine water. 13 ounces. <note>Gold "extinguished" or quenched refers to the alchemical process of heating the metal until red-hot and plunging it into a liquid to capture its "virtue."</note>

To 1 scruple of this preparation:
Nutmeg oil. 1 dram.
Clove oil. 1 dram.
The dose is half a scruple up to a whole scruple.
From the *Book of Preparations Concerning Gold*.

*Restorative medicines according to*
*Astronomical Anatomy.* <note>A Paracelsian concept where the human body is viewed as a "microcosm" reflecting the "macrocosm" of the stars and planets.</note>
There are fourfold bodies compared to the celestial anatomy, divided into two parts: two serving men, namely agarwood <note>original: "lignū aloes"</note> and Antimony; the other two for women, such as lemon balm and valerian.

*After a month has passed* <note>original: "Post mensem reuolutum"; likely referring to the completion of a lunar or menstrual cycle.</note>
For men, green oil of salt is suitable; for women, liquor of amber <note>original: "carabis," a medieval term for amber</note>: the preparations of which you can seek from the *Archidoxes* of Paracelsus. By these, the nature of the human body can be brought to a harmonic temperament, so that it is renewed and restored to the likeness of the celestial proportion.
From the *Great Surgery* of Paracelsus, volume 5, book 3, chapter 27.

102    **ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES**

...let water be distilled from vitriol <note>original: "vitriolo"; vitriol refers to various metal sulfates, like copper or iron sulfate, used in early chemistry and medicine</note> alone, and another from alum <note>a mineral salt used as an astringent</note> alone; let both waters be kept separately. To half a pound <note>The text uses the abbreviation "lb." for the Roman pound (libra) and "ß" for a half</note> of this water, add one and a half pounds of distilled vinegar and four pounds of egg whites boiled until hard. Distill each twice more, and a second time mixed together; then, for one pound of mercury, take three pounds of this final water. Once the dissolution is complete, it must be distilled again as many times as necessary until a yellow-colored powder appears. Finally, having poured on spirit of wine <note>original: "spiritū vini"; high-proof alcohol or ethanol</note>, let it be distilled as many times as seems fit. The dose is 3, 4, or 5 grains in a small amount of the best wine, or in an electuary <note>a medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</note> of roses, etc.

*The Great Preparation of Mercury.*

Take <note>The symbol ℞ stands for the Latin "Recipe," the traditional command to begin a medical prescription.</note> 1 pound of mercury or quicksilver; let this be thoroughly washed in vinegar in which salt has been dissolved. Afterwards, let it be mixed with half a pound of the purest salt and stirred in a marble mortar <note>original: "pila"</note> with a wooden pestle for as long as it takes until the salt turns completely black and has absorbed the quicksilver so that it no longer appears. When this is finished, let the salt be driven from the mercury through a retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids or gases</note> with its opening submerged in cold water, so that all of it, or the part that is pure, descends into the water. This can be repeated as many times as necessary until it appears perfectly purified. Finally, let them boil in the following lye <note>a strong alkaline solution used for cleaning or chemical reactions</note>. Take quicklime <note>original: "calcē viuam"; calcium oxide, a caustic chemical produced by heating limestone</note> and pour boiling water over it, stirring constantly until it cools. Once the sediment has settled, let the clear liquid be strained through a filter sleeve made in the manner of a Hippocratic sleeve <note>original: "sacci hypocratis"; a conical fabric bag used by early chemists and apothecaries for fine filtration</note>. This filtered liquid is added again to the ashes of woad <note>a plant (Isatis tinctoria) typically used for blue dye; its ashes were a source of potash</note> that have been thoroughly burnt...

Finally, let it be dissolved in that same Royal Water, and keep the solution set aside.

# 104 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.

...or distill seven times, each time pouring back the distilled liquid and that which has been sublimated <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid to purify it</term> onto its own dregs. After the final distillation, let whatever remains at the bottom be taken out, and the distilled liquid cast aside or set apart as a thing useless to the work. Let the ground-up material be placed in a pottery vessel and constantly stirred and moved with an iron rod over a modest fire of glowing coals, until it takes on a very deep red color and the virulence of the *aqua regia* <note>original: "aquæ regis"; literally "royal water," a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids used to dissolve noble metals like gold</note> completely vanishes. Finally, let this powder be washed with hot water as many times as necessary until it loses all its saltiness.

Afterward, let it be dried, and then let enough essence of wine <note>original: "eſſentiæ vini"; highly rectified or concentrated alcohol</note> of the highest perfection be poured over it so that it rises three or four fingers' breadth above the powder. Let it remain contained in a closed vessel placed among warm ashes for five or six days, shaking it three times each day. Then, finally, let the essence of wine be separated from the material by means of a water bath <term>balneum: also known as a bain-marie, a method of gentle heating using a vessel placed in boiling water</term>. Let the dried material be ground and then drenched with rose water mixed with musk, and let it sit in this for four or five days, disturbing the glass vessel by shaking it three times daily. At last, after the liquid has been drawn off by a water bath, the material of the "prepared mercury" will remain at the bottom; once removed from the glass, let it be kept in a very well-sealed container.

The dose is from 1 grain <term>grain: the smallest unit of weight in the apothecary system, roughly 65 milligrams</term> up to 3 grains—and even up to 1 dram <note>original: "ʒ. j."; though a dram (about 3.9 grams) is a very high dose for mercury, the text suggests adjusting based on the patient</note> depending on the person and the disease. It should be administered by mouth for internal diseases in theriacal or mithridatic water.

Administered by mouth, it expels through sweats and radically cures hidden ulcerations in the throat, bladder, and kidneys.

*Sweet essence of Mercury.*

Let it be dissolved in water of saltpeter, and reduced by distillation into a white powder; this should be placed in a melting vessel over glowing coals, and <term>strong water</term><note>original: "aqua fortis"; nitric acid.</note> drawn from vitriol and alum should be poured over it and then drawn off. Let the powder be washed several times with distilled common water so that it loses its <term>acrimony</term><note>The harsh, burning, or acidic quality of the chemical.</note>. Afterwards, pour on burning water and draw it off by distillation; repeat this ten times. Finally, with a very strong fire so that the vessel almost glows, draw off nearly all the moisture, so that the mercury powder turns red like blood and is perceived to be very sweet to the taste.

*Preparation of Mercury.*

Let it be dissolved in strong water, to which one part of melted salt and <unclear>la-</unclear>

### The Care of Internal Ulcers

Take: **Mastic** <note>original: "masticis"; a resin from the mastic tree, used to strengthen the stomach and dry up excess moisture.</note>, half a drachm; **Hermodactyls** <note>original: "hermodactilorum"; a root used historically to treat joint pain and "phlegmatic" conditions.</note>, half a drachm.

110 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES
*Various types of mercurial essences and mineral*
*laxatives, in order.*

First,

Vitriolated sulfur. Liquefied vitriol <note>original: "Vitriolum". In early medicine, vitriol referred to various sulfate minerals, such as copper (blue) or iron (green) sulfates, used for their drying and caustic properties.</note>.
Second.
Mercurial Seua <note>A Paracelsian term referring to a specific vital essence or potent extract of a mineral.</note>, laxative Chimolea <note>likely referring to Cimolean earth or a refined mineral clay used in medicinal preparations.</note>.
Third.
Mercury of metals. Precipitated and extracted mercury of minerals.

*Definitions of the Mercuries, in the*
*order of the preceding list.*

Vitriolated sulfur is a certain extraction of sulfur from vitriol by means of decoction <note>The process of boiling down a substance to extract its medicinal essence.</note>. Its dosage is the same as that of black hellebore <note>A powerful and potentially toxic plant-based purgative; the comparison suggests this mineral preparation was also very potent.</note>. Liquefied vitriol is that which is drawn from copper and is not solidified any further. Mercurial Seua is a liquid drawn from alums <note>Aluminum-based mineral salts.</note> in which mercury is generated.
Laxative chimolea is a powder extracted from the <term>flowers <note>In alchemy, "flowers" refers to the fine, powdery sublimate created when a substance is heated and then condenses directly into a solid.</note></term> of mineral salts.
Precipitated mercury of metals is the reduction of metals into their "first matter" <note>The "Materia Prima," the fundamental, undifferentiated substance from which all metals were believed to originate in alchemical theory.</note>, and from there, its precipitation back into itself. Mercury of minerals is an oily substance <note>original: "oleitas"</note> drawn from the minerals of gold and silver.
Mercurial- <note>This is a catchword, indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

BOOK ONE. 109
*The three bodies of Mercury.* <note>In alchemy, mercury (quicksilver) was often viewed in three distinct states of maturity or purity.</note>

*First.*
Before it bursts forth from its mineral matrix <note>the "womb" or surrounding rock in which a metal forms</note> and earth; it must be taken along with its "body" <note>the raw ore</note> and prepared for use in the mercurial art.

*Second.*
After it has been "born"—that is, when it has been separated from its body by a medium heat.

*Third.*
When it has been prepared by the alchemical art and reduced into the form of a "burnt" metal <note>original: *adusti metalli*; likely referring to a calcined or oxidized state</note>.

*The First Preparation of Mercury.*
Take <note>original: ♃, a symbol often used here as a shorthand for "Recipe" or "Take"</note> 20 pounds of the mineral ore in which Mercury is born, found in its first stage of thickening <term>coagulation: the process of a liquid or vapor turning into a solid</term>, before it has hardened or fully formed into a solid mass. Let it be ground and boiled in rainwater for one hour. The impurities will separate to the surface on their own; remove these by tilting the vessel. Let the sediment that remains—the "body"—be dried, ground, and passed through a sieve. Afterward, you shall distill it from a gourd-shaped flask <term>cucurbit: a rounded glass or clay vessel used in distillation</term> over a very intense fire. A very clear, white liquid will rise; save this, and separate the watery part <term>phlegm: the tasteless, watery byproduct of distillation</term> from it using a water bath <term>balneum maris: a "bath of Mary" or double-boiler, used for gentle heating</term>. At the bottom, a certain heavy oiliness will remain, which is slightly less than the mercury itself. Use two grains of this "juice" of the immature mineral and six grains of oil of spike <note>an essential oil derived from *Lavandula latifolia*</note>, following the standard mercurial practice.

*The Second Preparation.*
Take solid Mercury and create an amalgam <note>a blend of mercury and another metal</note> with an equal weight of tin. Let them melt together into water <note>The text cuts off here as it describes the transition of the metals into a liquid state.</note>

<summary>This page categorizes the three states of mercury (raw ore, separated, and chemically altered) and provides a detailed recipe for extracting a "heavy oil" from immature mercury ore using rainwater and distillation.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, alchemy, distillation, mineral matrix, oil of spike, cucurbit, balneum maris, tin amalgam</keywords>

BOOK ONE. 113

### Preparation of a cleansing ointment among others,
### for corroding and cleansing.

**Take:** Wild honey, 1 pound.
<term>Hepatic aloe</term> <note>original: "Aloe epatici"; a liver-colored grade of aloe resin used for its purgative and healing properties</note>, 1 ½ ounces.
Burnt alum, extinguished with vinegar several times, 1 ounce.
Egg yolks, 20 in number.
Turpentine, ½ pound.

Stir all the ingredients together into a paste, and cook slowly in a clean pan until it takes the form of a blackish or chestnut-colored <term>plaster</term> <note>a thick, tacky medicinal substance applied to the skin</note>. Use this to draw out poison.

### Drawing ointment.
**Take:** 20 fresh fir cones. Boil them in water to extract the resin; separate the resin while boiling and add an equal amount of turpentine, and half as much <term>colophony</term> <note>original: "resinæ"; likely referring to the hardened resin used to thicken the mixture</note> as the turpentine. Let them boil together and use.

### Another for the same purpose.
**Take:** Gum <term>Opopanax</term> <note>a gum resin used historically to treat inflammation</note>, ½ ounce.
<term>Sagapenum</term> <note>original: "Serapini"; a resin from a plant in the fennel family</note>, Galbanum, and Ammoniac, 2 ounces each.
<term>Bdellium</term> <note>a semi-transparent resin similar to myrrh</note>, 6 ounces.
Dissolve these in vinegar and strain them. Add 2 ounces of rhubarb, ½ ounce of the roots of non-lethal <term>Ephemerum</term> <note>likely referring to a species of Iris or similar plant; the author specifies "non-lethal" to distinguish it from more toxic varieties like Meadow Saffron</note>, and ½ ounce of <term>amber</term> <note>original: "carabi"</note>. When ground up, let them be reduced to a plaster and use in the usual manner.

114    ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES
*The cure for a uterine mole.* <note>original: "Molæ matricis cura." In early medicine, a "mole" referred to a fleshy, non-viable mass in the uterus that could mimic the symptoms of pregnancy, such as a fibroid or a molar pregnancy.</note>

Its treatment is twofold: the first is by expulsion,
the other by preservation.
*A remedy for expulsion*
Take 4 drachms <note>The symbol ʒ represents a drachm, a unit of weight roughly equal to 3.9 grams.</note> of oriental saffron.
Take half a drachm <note>The symbol ß or ss denotes "half."</note> each of Borax
and Amber <note>original: "Carabe."</note>.
6 drachms of Scammony <note>A resinous juice used as a powerful purgative.</note>.
1 drachm of Lapis lazuli.
Make a pessary <note term="pessary">A medicinal substance shaped for insertion into the vagina.</note> with thickened opoponax <note>A vegetable gum resin used in pharmacy.</note>.
It must be noted that this kind of medicine
cannot be used safely at all unless the mole
is still small, so that it may pass through the womb
before the true term of birth. If you attempt
to expel a large mole, you will expose the woman to death.
*A preservative remedy for a mole.*
Take 1 drachm of agaric troches <note term="troches">Small medicinal lozenges or cakes.</note>.
Half a drachm of Euphorbium <note>A caustic resin from the spurge plant.</note>.
Filtered and thickened opoponax to the weight
of all the other ingredients. Make a pessary, and use it before
the mole becomes large. And if it does not cease after one night,
you shall stop the medicine, because it is a sign
of the generation of offspring <note>The author suggests that if the treatment does not cause a discharge or change, the woman may actually be pregnant with a child rather than carrying a mole.</note>.
Book on Apostemes <note term="aposteme">A word for an internal swelling, abscess, or tumor.</note>, chapter 57.

*The cure for mulberry-shaped growths.* <note>original: "Mori cura." This likely refers to "morus," a term for skin growths or hemorrhoids that resemble mulberries.</note>

Take 1 drachm of Juniper oil.
7 drachms of Oil of Spike <note>Lavender oil.</note>.
7 drachms of Oil of Bricks <note term="Oil of Bricks">A traditional remedy made by heating oil-soaked bricks and collecting the distillate.</note>.
Let it be made.

## Treatment for the bites of a mad dog:
   Let a strong purging be performed without delay through the mouth and through the bowels, using <term>colocynth</term> <note>a powerful purgative derived from the "bitter apple" fruit</note>, or <term>agaric</term> <note>a fungus found on larch trees, used as a laxative</note>, <term>spurge</term>, the juice of elder bark, rhubarb, etc.
   Externally, the wound should be cooled with <term>spermiolum</term> <note>likely "frog spawn oil," a common cooling agent in early modern medicine</note>, or frog spawn, oil of mandrake, and the punctured wounds should be treated with <term>stictic</term> <note>styptic or healing</note> plasters, in which there is no **Greek pitch** <note>original: "pix græca"; a common term for rosin or colophony</note>.
   If the madness <note>original: "rabies"</note> does not relent or lessen, an opiate should be administered according to customary use, for it takes away the madness.
   In this case, the best remedy would be sulfur extracted from <term>vitriol</term> <note>metal sulfates, such as iron or copper sulfate</note> through the process of <term>calcination</term> <note>heating a substance to high temperatures to change its chemical properties</note> and preparation.
   *From The Great Surgery, Part 1, Book 3, Chapter 1.*

Treatment for Nacta.

<term>Nacta</term> <note>a term used in Paracelsian medicine for a specific type of swelling</note> is an abscess of the breasts in women, occurring when immature menstrual flows are mixed with milk.

Opodeltoch.
Take <term>Opoponax</term> <note>a gum resin used in traditional medicine</note>.
<term>Bdellium</term> <note>a semi-transparent oleo-gum resin</note>—one ounce of each.
<term>Sagapenum</term> <note>original: "Serapini"; a gum resin from an Eastern Mediterranean plant</note>—2 ounces.
Mix these with enough liquid vinegar, strain through a cloth, and boil down to the thickness of honey, and then add:
<term>Mummy</term> <note>original: "Mummiae"; a substance derived from embalmed bodies or bitumen, believed to possess great healing power for the vital spirit</note>—1 ounce.
Round <term>Birthwort</term> <note>original: "Aristolochiæ rotundæ"; a medicinal plant often used for wounds and childbirth</note>—3 ounces.
Liquid of <term>Cachimia</term> <note>an alchemical term for mineral dross or residue from smelting</note>—as much as all the other ingredients combined, and let it be made into a plaster.
*The Lesser Surgery, Book 3, Chapter 3.*

*Another form of "stictic" plaster for wounds and punctured ulcers.*
<note>The term "stictic" comes from the Greek word for "punctured," indicating this plaster was specifically formulated for deep, narrow wounds like those from a blade or needle.</note>

Take:
<term>Galbanum</term>,

Let all these be ground very finely, placed in a glazed earthenware pot, and soaked in strong wine vinegar; let them macerate for a natural day <note>a full 24-hour period</note>. Afterward, let them be cooked over a gentle charcoal fire so that the gums gradually melt. Once this is achieved, pour them into a bag to be strained, squeezed out, and separated from the dregs, which are to be discarded. Let this strained liquid boil again in the pot until the vinegar has evaporated, stirring constantly so that the gums do not happen to burn; keep it aside for a while. Afterward, take two pounds of olive oil and half a pound of new wax, which, when placed in the pot, should melt gradually. Finally, throw in one and a half pounds of <term>litharge</term> <note>lead oxide</note> ground very finely, and let everything be stirred together...

126    ON CURING DISEASES.
**Oil from Saturn.** <note>original: "Oleum ex Saturno." In alchemy, Saturn is the symbolic name for the metal lead.</note>

Lead is burnt into a yellow ash <note>original: "calcem flauam," likely referring to litharge or lead monoxide</note>, and covered with distilled vinegar so that the liquid rises three fingers' breadth above it. It is cooked over a fire, then set aside to settle. The clear vinegar is poured off, and fresh vinegar is added; this process is repeated until all the sweetness of the lead passes into the vinegar. <note>Lead acetate is famously sweet-tasting, historically known as "sugar of lead," though it is highly toxic.</note> This collected sweet vinegar is distilled in a <term>water bath</term> <note>original: "balneo maris," a double boiler system used for gentle heating</note>; a purple sweet matter remains at the bottom. Let it be covered with distilled <term>heavenly water</term> <note>original: "aqua cœlefti," which usually refers to highly rectified wine-spirit or pure rainwater</note> and allowed to boil until a white foam floats on top. This foam is collected. Then, the remaining matter is boiled again over the fire, and when it releases more white foam, it is collected and set aside with the rest. This is done until the purple matter at the bottom stops foaming, at which point the residue is thrown away. The collected white foam is dried of the remaining vinegar in a water bath until a white substance settles at the bottom. This is dissolved again by pouring on <term>strong water</term> <note>original: "aquæ fortis," the historical name for nitric acid</note>, and the liquid is drawn off in a water bath. Fresh liquid is poured on again and drawn off like before. After doing this three or four times, the matter will become finer and whiter. To this, heavenly water is poured once more and drawn off in a water bath five or six times until all the sharp acidity has left the material and the salt of Saturn. In a damp place, it dissolves by <note>original: "per fluxionem," referring to deliquescence, where a substance absorbs moisture from the air to become liquid</note> into a water or oil, used for all defects.

**Cure for bone fractures.**
Crushed and cooked <term>comfrey</term> <note>original: "Symphitum." Also known as "knitbone," comfrey was the primary herbal remedy for fractures in the early modern period.</note> placed upon the injury, heals.

*Cure for Whitlow or "the Worm."*

A whitlow <note>original: *panaritius*; a painful infection of the fingertip</note> or "the worm" <note>In early medicine, painful, throbbing infections were often attributed to "worms" eating the tissue.</note> arises from the very substance from which the nails are formed, and it settles in the <term>extremities</term> <note>original: *locustis*; here referring to the joints or the ends of the digits</note>, such as the fingers and toes. The cure is threefold: the first is performed through <term>sigils</term> <note>original: *caracteres*; symbolic or magical inscriptions believed to hold healing power</note>, as is the custom in Carinthia <note>A mountainous region in modern-day southern Austria.</note>; the second is through superstition; the third is through natural medicines, such as a numbing ointment made from <term>frog spawn</term> <note>The gelatinous eggs of frogs were frequently used in medieval and Renaissance pharmacy as a cooling agent to draw out heat and inflammation.</note>. *Little Surgery*, Book 3, Chapter 3.

### Balsam or Oil of Lead, also called Saturn
<note>In alchemy, Saturn is the traditional name for the metal lead.</note>

From the ashes of this metal, a spirit is extracted using distilled vinegar. Once this "sweetness" <note>likely referring to lead acetate, known as "sugar of lead" for its sweet taste</note> has turned into an oil, it is a supreme remedy not only for ulcers, but also for abscesses and wounds.

# 264 ON THE CURING OF DISEASES.

*External remedy for ulcers.*

Take: One pound each of balsam prepared for wounds (according to the process for external remedies in the *Archidoxes*) made from <term>Sameth|A term used in Paracelsian alchemy, often referring to a specific preparation of tartar or a secret mineral salt</term>, and balsam made from <term>rust|In this context, 'rubigo' or rust refers to the oxidation of metals, particularly iron, processed alchemically for its drying and healing properties</term>. Once these have been mixed into one, add half a pound of oil of iron. Let all these, mixed together, be applied over the ulcers; let them be washed daily as shall seem expedient, and let the consolidative <note>original: *consolidativum*; a healing plaster intended to close and firm up a wound</note> plaster be applied, which we have taught for ulcers.

Therefore, you shall continue the bath treatments until the very end of the healing process. The limbs are to be very well compressed with bandages, for the suppression of concurrent <note>original: *accidentium*; referring to the secondary symptoms or complications that arise alongside the main disease</note> symptoms.

*External remedy for blemishes.*

Above, we have sufficiently explained with a specific <term>corrosive|A substance that wears away or 'eats' the skin, used by early doctors to remove diseased or discolored tissue</term> the method by which the skin and blemishes are to be removed; but now, let us hear by what remedy the skin may be brought back over its flesh.

Take: Balsam for the ulcer, prepared according to the teaching of the *Archidoxes* regarding external remedies. Take equal parts of washed turpentine, oil of <term>earthworms|Commonly used in early modern medicine for their supposed ability to soothe nerves and restore suppleness to the skin</term>, and oil of eggs. With these, you shall wash all the flesh that has been stripped of its skin. After this care, nothing further is required. The property of this medicine is such that it brings about a new color with new skin, and a natural hardness <note>meaning a healthy firmness or durability of the skin</note>, so that it cannot be further contaminated by the preceding blemishes.

Book of the *Archidoxes*, on external remedies.

*Wound-*

->*The form of the Fifth Essence of Gold.*<-

Take as much gold as you wish, but let it be first purified with royal cement <note>a refining process where gold is heated with salts to remove base metals</note> or antimony <note>a metalloid used in smelting to separate gold from silver and copper</note>. From this gold, you are to remove the metallic property or its malleability <note>the physical quality that allows it to be hammered into sheets</note> using salt water. Afterward, wash the residue with fresh water. Finally, extract its tincture <note>the concentrated essence or "soul" of the material, often appearing as a deep color</note> through spirit of wine <note>high-proof distilled alcohol</note>. At last, by separating the spirit from it through elevation <note>the process of distillation or evaporation</note>, the fifth essence <term>Quintessence: the purest, most potent, and spiritual core of a substance, stripped of its physical dross</term> will remain at the bottom of the vessel.

->*The formula for Salt Water.*<-

Take the cleanest and whitest salt, produced by nature and not by art <note>natural rock or sea salt rather than a chemically manufactured version</note>. It should be ground and melted several times, and ground again. You shall then distill it after it has been mixed and dissolved with the juice of radish roots. Again, mix it with equal weights of bloodwort <note>original: *sanguineæ*; likely refers to the plant *Cornus sanguinea* or another herb with red sap used as a reagent</note> while it is still green, and distill it five times. In this liquid, gold in the form of thin plates is dissolved into that powder which we mentioned above. Let it be washed frequently with clear distilled water until all the saltiness is gone; it is easily separated because the liquid does not mix with the gold in its depths. Therefore, once the corrosive <note>the acidic salt solution</note> is removed, the gold remains through

*The fifth essence of marcasites.* <note>In early chemistry, "marcasite" was a general term for various metallic ores and minerals, including what we now call pyrite.</note>

Our process and method of extracting the fifth essence from marcasites is in all ways similar to that used for metals. As for our statement that the fifth essence represents the supreme powers and virtues of things—as is also said of the secret <term>arcana</term>, <note>original: "arcanis"; the hidden, transformative powers within nature</note> masteries, elixirs, and so on—let us put it this way: these secrets are even greater than those essences. The reason is this: these "arcana" contain within themselves the fifth essences, and furthermore, they are brought to such a state of subtlety and sharpness that they receive a power far greater than a simple fifth essence, because of their appropriate and specific qualities.

Take one pound <note>original: "℔.j."</note> of any marcasite, very finely ground. Take two pounds <note>original: "℔.ij."</note> of corrosive water <note>likely a strong acid such as nitric acid</note>. Once mixed together,

...stay in digestion for two or three months. They will be reduced into a liquid: distill this entirely by fire, and vapor and oil will rise. *The Quintessence of Salts*

Once the salts have been taken, you shall calcine <term>calcine|To heat a substance to high temperatures in order to reduce it to a powder or change its chemical properties.</term> them thoroughly; if they are volatile, simply scorch them. Afterward, let them be dissolved into a thin state and distilled into water. Place this water into putrefaction <note>In alchemy, putrefaction refers to the breakdown and "ripening" of a substance through gentle, sustained heat.</note> for a month and distill it by means of a water bath <note>original: "balneum," likely a "Bain-marie" or double boiler setup.</note>. Sweet water will rise; discard this. When no more [water] wishes to rise, place it back into digestion for another month and distill as before, and do this as many times as necessary until no more sweetness is perceived, but only saltiness. In this way, you will have the quintessence of the salt at the bottom; from a burnt pound, you will get barely two ounces. However, one must proceed differently with alum and vitriol <term>vitriol|A historical name for various sulfate minerals, such as iron or copper sulfate, highly valued for their corrosive and medicinal properties.</term>, because they do not allow themselves to be calcined to a flow in the same way that salts are brought to.

->*The Fifth Essence from precious stones, gems, and pearls.*<-

Take gems, pearls, or "unions" <note>original: "vnionibus"; a historical term for large, high-quality pearls found singly rather than in clusters</note>, and let them be crushed into coarse fragments—not ground into a fine powder. Place these in a glass vessel and pour "radical vinegar" <term>radical vinegar: a highly concentrated, distilled vinegar used by alchemists as a powerful solvent</term> over them so that it stands four or five fingers deep above the fragments. Let them be digested in horse manure <note>Alchemists used the natural heat generated by fermenting horse manure as a steady, low-temperature "water bath" for long-term extraction</note> for an entire month.

When this time has passed, the entire material will have become liquid. You shall wash this liquid with more fresh radical vinegar and stir them together; let them sit so that the vinegar absorbs the pigment of the stone. Pour this colored liquid into a glass, and pour more vinegar onto the remaining material. Repeat this process until the vinegar is no longer colored by the stones. The fifth essence <term>fifth essence: also known as the "quintessence," this was considered the most purified and potent form of a substance, stripped of its gross physical impurities</term> is contained within these colored extracts.

146 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES
*The Quintessence of combustible substances.*

Combustible bodies are those which are not edible, containing pitch, oil, or resin within them, such as terebinth <note>original: "terebintus"; refers to the turpentine tree or the resin derived from it</note>, fir, juniper, cypress, and other things subject to burning.

Take similar materials which, when ground, fill a strong, glazed earthen pot. Cover it and seal it with the <term>lute of wisdom|lutum sapientiae, a specialized airtight sealant made of clay, dung, and hair used by alchemists to prevent vapors from escaping</term> so that it cannot breathe. Burn it with a circular fire <note>ignis circulatorius, a heat source that surrounds the entire vessel</note> for twenty-four hours, ensuring it remains at a steady heat. Once the pot is glowing like hot coals, remove it from the fire and let the material be nourished in manure for four weeks. Afterwards, let whatever is able to rise be distilled. Place this in a <term>horse’s belly|venter equinus, a warm, moist environment created by burying a vessel in fermenting horse manure to provide a gentle, consistent heat</term> to distill all the humidity from it, and return it to putrefy until no more moisture comes out. Finally, the quintessence <note>the "fifth essence," or the most purified and potent form of a substance</note> of that which you took remains at the bottom.

*The Quintessence from growing things.*

Take: Herbs, leaves, etc. Let them be ground thoroughly and, stored in a vessel suitable for this purpose, let them be placed in horse manure for four weeks. Afterwards, distill them using a <term>bath|balneum, usually a water bath or bain-marie</term>, and place them again in horse manure for eight days, and once more let them be distilled through a sea-bath <note>balneum maris</note>. The quintessence will rise through the <term>alembic|the cooling cap of a still used to collect and condense purified vapors</term> and the "body" <note>the solid, depleted remains</note> will stay at the bottom. If any of the quintessence remains at the bottom with the dregs, let them putrefy as before and be distilled. To the distilled water, add the growing material once again, and... <note>the text ends here mid-instruction</note>

...and let this be digested through a pelican <note>A "pelican" is a specialized alchemical vessel with two side-tubes that circulate vapors back into the liquid, used for long-term "digestion" or circular distillation.</note> for six days; from this will come a thick color. Draw this off using a water bath <note>original: "balneum maris" (bath of Mary), a gentle heating method where the vessel sits in hot water rather than over a direct flame.</note>, and the "body" <note>The physical residue or bulk of the material.</note> will recede, leaving the quintessence <term>quintessence: literally the "fifth essence," the most concentrated and purified form of a substance, believed to contain its soul or medicinal power.</term> remaining at the bottom. *The quintessence of spices or fragrant things.*

Take musk, civet <note>A pungent, fatty secretion from the civet cat, highly prized in early perfumery and medicine.</note>, camphor, and similar things; let them be mixed with oil of sweet almonds, and once stored in glass, let them be digested in the sun for the appropriate time until they are reduced into a paste. Afterward, let them be squeezed out from their dregs. In this way, the "body" is separated from the quintessence, which, being mixed with the oil, shall be separated as follows:

Take rectified "burning wine" <note>original: "vinum ardens," an early alchemical term for distilled alcohol or ethanol.</note>, with which the said oil is to be drenched, and let them be left in digestion for six days. Afterward, let them be distilled through ashes <note>A method of dry heating using a bed of hot ash to regulate temperature.</note>; the burning wine will ascend, and the quintessence with it, while the oil remains at the bottom, without any substance of the quintessence. Finally, let this burning wine be distilled through a water bath, and the quintessence will remain at the bottom, separated in the form of an oil.

148      ON CURING DISEASES
*The quintessence <term>Quintessence: In alchemical theory, this is the "fifth essence," the most pure and potent spiritual core of a physical substance, separated from the four elements.</term> from edible and drinkable things.*

Take edible material cut up very finely; let it boil for three days in a closed and sealed <note>original: "lutatoque" — sealed with "lute," a specialized alchemical paste or clay used to make vessels airtight.</note> jar. Afterward, let it be squeezed through a linen cloth or a sieve, and then distilled by a water bath <term>Water bath: Also called a "balneum maris," this is a method of gentle heating using a vessel placed in boiling water to ensure the substance does not burn.</term>. First, a certain watery liquid rises; after this has all risen, the quintessence will be left at the bottom. This is the supreme nourishment above all others.

*On the drinkable.*

Take any drinkable thing as it is with its whole substance; store this in a pelican <term>Pelican: A circulating alchemical flask with two curved "arms" that return distilled vapors to the base, named for its resemblance to a bird pecking its own breast.</term> and let it be digested in horse manure <note>Rotting horse manure was used as a natural, steady source of gentle heat for long-term alchemical "digestion."</note> for a month—though it will be better for a year or more. You will find a certain digested thing in the pelican. Separate this first by a water bath, then afterward by ashes <note>A "sand" or ash bath allowed for higher temperatures than water but more control than a direct flame.</note>, and finally by fire. In this way, you will have three quintessences which are present in all drinkable things, for many reasons as we have established regarding their generations. Let each be kept separately in glass; let the latter two be further digested and placed in a water bath, and more of the first quintessence will rise. Do this as many times as necessary until no more of the first rises. In this way, they are most excellently separated.

*Cure for a Ranula,*

A <term>ranula</term><note>original: "Ranula"; literally "little frog," referring to a cyst or swelling under the tongue that resembles a frog's underbelly.</note> is a certain abscess accumulated from the veins that are under the tongue, taking its origin from the root of the tongue. The cure:
Let the mentioned small veins be cut, and let the blood flow out from them.
Afterward, let them be washed thoroughly with waters of <term>Adder’s Tongue</term><note>original: "Braſſatellæ"</note>, <term>Lady’s Mantle</term><note>original: "alchimillæ"</note>, <term>Sanicle</term><note>original: "diapenſiæ"</note>, <term>Agrimony</term>, etc., or with oil of <term>St. John’s Wort</term><note>original: "ypericonis"</note>, or oil of the flowers of <term>Centaury</term>. If it should return, the same treatment is repeated. If the ranula is small, let it be washed more frequently with the waters, etc.
*Paracelsus’s Surgery*, Book 3, chapter 3.

*Restraining the flow of blood*
*from a vein*

These are the things that stop bleeding:
<term>Saffron of Mars</term><note>original: "Crocus martis"; a chemical term for iron oxide, processed through "reverberation" or intense heat in a furnace.</note> very finely reverberated.
Burnt copper prepared in the manner of "saffron" <note>i.e., reduced to a fine reddish-brown powder</note>.
Flour which adheres to the walls of mills, sprinkled on wounds until it thickens the blood into a paste.
The hairs growing under the tails of hares.

A vein that is not constricted by these means—and especially by the ones mentioned previously—will be difficult and dangerous to compress, unless a proper amount of blood has first flowed out. It should be noted that blood flows most abundantly from the wounded due to an abundance of choler <term>choler: also known as yellow bile, this humor was associated with heat and aggression; an excess was thought to make the blood "boil" and flow more rapidly</term>. This flow cannot be stopped by any remedy unless the choler is calmed; if it is stopped by force, it is to be feared that the blood might be forced to flow internally within the body, which would be extremely dangerous. Therefore, one must wait until a natural calming of the symptoms occurs.

## Explanation

Now the opportunity arises to discuss the furnace used for preparing calcined <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to oxidize it or reduce it to powder</term> and reverberated materials. Two furnaces are constructed in the shape of those in which bread is usually baked, or perhaps slightly lower on the inside. Between these two, another intermediate furnace is built, but one foot lower, equipped with a grate. In this middle furnace, a flaming fire is built using very dry wood, so that the flame has no vent in that central furnace, but instead passes into the side furnaces through specific vents. In this way, the flame may reverberate <note>original: *reuerberet*; in a reverberatory furnace, the heat is reflected or "reverberated" off the roof onto the material, rather than the fire touching the material directly</note> around the vessels containing the material. The heat then exits through other vents on the opposite side of each lateral furnace; otherwise, the flame and heat would not enter. The vessels containing the material...

<margin>k 4</margin> <note>This is a printer's signature mark, used to help the bookbinder assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

***Water or Oil of Salt.***

Let common salt be liquefied by fire, into which you should throw glowing fragments of brick; then drive it through a retort <note>A glass vessel with a long, curved neck used for distilling liquids</note> to dissolve metals.

***Or:***

A hollowed-out radish is filled with salt, and the salt is allowed to dissolve into water in a humid place. Glowing fragments of brick are extinguished in this water as described above, and it is distilled in the same manner.

***Or:***

It is mixed with an equal weight of saltpeter <term>nitri: potassium nitrate, used here as a powerful reagent</term>, then dried and liquefied together, and distilled into a spirit.

***Or:***

It is sprinkled frequently with vinegar and dried, then distilled with a most vehement fire into a spirit.

***Or:***

Mixed with dew, it is distilled for the purpose of dissolving gold and for curing various diseases.

*The First Essence of Gems.*

Take one ounce of emeralds or other gems, very finely ground. Calcine them with dissolved salt until they are turned to whiteness. Continue this until it falls from its spiritual nature and condition to the bottom like a "body," and like a liquid of metals or of honey.

*Treatment for Sephirus.*
<note>Sephirus (or Sephiros) is a term used in medieval medicine, derived from Arabic sources, to describe a hard, non-painful tumor or swelling that does not produce pus.</note>

Sephirus is a hard abscess produced from dry salt <note>referring to an excess of "salty" humors in the body</note>; it does not come to a head <note>original: "matureseceus," meaning it does not ripen or form pus</note>, is tinged with many colors, and is curable only by resolution <note>the process of the swelling dissolving and being reabsorbed into the body rather than bursting</note>.

Take: Juniper mistletoe 1/2 pound.
Fenugreek 4 ounces.
Waters of Eryngo <note>Sea Holly</note> and 
Sharp Dock, 2 ounces each.
Marshmallow juice as much as is sufficient for incorporation.

-># BOOK ONE. 171<-

And continue until small veins <note>These "veins" refer to the streaks of condensation forming inside the glass during distillation.</note> are seen stretching to the middle of the helmet <note>The "galea" or "head" of the distilling apparatus.</note>; then, having removed the receiver, let another be attached, and draw off as much of the liquid as before. Finally, having removed this receiver and attached another, let that which remains be drawn off. Keep these three types of distilled liquids separate. The remaining wine should then be distilled through ashes <note>A "cineres" or ash-bath, used to provide gentle, even heat.</note> until a thick and black material remains at the bottom of the vessel. Pour the third liquid (which was drawn off last) onto this material, draw it off, and pour it back again; continue this until the material acquires the thickness of honey. When placed in a damp spot, it will produce small red stones; remove these and save them. Pour the third liquid over the material once more and cook it as before, continuing until no more small stones are produced. 

The collected spirits should be washed with the phlegm <note>The watery, less potent byproduct of distillation.</note> and, having poured on the liquid from the second distillation, let them be digested and dried as many times as necessary until they turn white. Finally, macerate them with the liquid from the first distillation and separate them until they become volatile. 

An amalgam of gold <note>original: "solis" (of the sun), the alchemical name for gold</note> and mercury should be burned with sulfur; wash the resulting powder <term>calx: a fine powder produced by the oxidation or "burning" of a metal</term> with warm distilled water. Take half an ounce <note>original symbol: ℥.ß.</note> of this powder with one and a half ounces <note>original symbol: ℥.j.ß.</note> of the small stones and let them digest in rectified aqua vitae <note>Concentrated alcohol, literally "water of life."</note> for fifteen days. From an upright cucurbit <note>A gourd-shaped glass distilling flask.</note>, draw off one part, and the "water of gold" will ascend, tinted with a saffron color. Add fresh stones to the dregs again, allow them to digest, and distill as many times as needed until all the gold is resolved into a spirit.

->### Spirit of Vitriol.<-

Distill it nine times with a strong fire in an upright cucurbit.

172 ->ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES<-

using a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation</note>, always <term>cohobating</term> <note>original: "cohobãdo"; the process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its residue to extract more essence</note>, or redistilling it over the dregs. Finally, with the most intense fire, let all the spirits be extracted through a <term>retort</term> <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note>. In this manner, you will have a most potent spirit of <term>vitriol</term> <note>sulfuric acid, often derived from iron or copper sulfates</note>.

->*Oil of Colcothar*<- <note>original: "Oleum colcotarinum"; Colcothar is the red iron oxide residue left after the distillation of vitriol</note>
From red colcothar, crushed by itself or mixed with salt and distilled wine, if you wish, through the most intense fire, a dark red oil will flow out from a reclining jar; this is called oil of vitriol.
Lastly, the salt called <term>alkali</term> is extracted from the dregs of the vitriol. When <insert>the leaves of the horizon</insert> <note>original: "foliis orizõtis"; likely an alchemical reference to gold leaf or a specific mineral preparation</note> are added to the oil and digested with it, it becomes "foliated" oil of vitriol.

->*Vegetable Spirit from Wine*<-
Let the most noble wine be contained in a very large glass vessel, so that it occupies barely a fourth part of the capacity. Let it be <term>digested</term> <note>warmed gently to promote a chemical reaction</note> in horse dung <note>a common method for providing steady, low heat</note> or a water bath for 30 days, or until a liquor resembling the form of oil is seen upon the wine. Here, with the fire greatly reduced, let it be separated until the spirit ceases to rise. Then, let it be digested again in a water bath for 24 days, so that from one pound of wine, barely one <term>scruple</term> <note>a small unit of weight, roughly 1.3 grams</note> of such spirit is obtained. It is most useful for the production of long life, and also for extracting <term>tinctures</term>.

->*The Gargle*<-
Take 4 ounces of honey of roses.

### 176 ON THE TREATMENT OF DISEASES.
Treatment of <term>scrofula</term> <note>original: "strumarum"; referring to chronic swellings of the lymph nodes, typically associated with tuberculosis</note>.

Take 4 ounces of <term>tutia</term> <note>a mineral form of crude zinc oxide, frequently used in historical medicine for skin and eye ailments</note>.
Take 6 ounces each of fused salt and <term>quicklime</term> <note>calcium oxide, a caustic alkaline substance produced by heating limestone</note>.
Let these be placed in layers and subjected to the third degree of heat <note>in early chemistry, heat was categorized by "degrees," with the third being quite intense, often a strong fire or hot coals</note>; from this, the <term>alkali</term> is to be extracted.
There may be added:
To this prepared tutia,
Take 1 ounce each of <term>salt anathron</term> <note>likely natron or mineral soda</note>, also known as gall-salt, and fused salt.
Take 15 ounces of distilled urine.
Let a mixture be made. It is to be given as a drink in the morning and the evening for 3 or 4 weeks.
From the *Book of Preparations*, regarding Tutia.

### Oil of sulfur.

Take 1 pound of ground sulfur and 2 pounds of crushed flints. Let them be mixed and distilled from a <term>retort</term> <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> using an entirely slow fire in a very well-sealed <note>original: "lutatissimo"; referring to the use of 'lute,' a clay-like paste used to seal joints in alchemical apparatus to prevent leaks</note> vessel. This is a remedy for fevers, wounds, and ulcers.

### Oil of sulfur.

Take half a pound of sulfur.
Take 4 ounces of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>ammonium chloride, a salt used to help dissolve or react with other minerals</note>.
Take 1 pound of quicklime.
Let all be mixed together after being ground, and pour over them water in which a little salt has been dissolved. Finally, let the oil be distilled from a retort and separated from the <term>phlegm</term> <note>the watery or watery-vapor part of the distillation that separates from the oily essence</note>.

->**The Quintessence of Sulfur**<-

Take a half-pound <note>Original: ℔.ß. The ℔ symbol represents a libra or pound; ß represents a half.</note> of sulfur flowers.

->**The Composition of Penetrating Aqua Vitae**<-

Take one pound each of the distilled waters of lemon balm, roses, wallflower <note>Original: cheiri</note>, sage, and balsam. Take an ounce <note>Original: ℥</note> each of all types of pepper, cubebs, ginger, cinnamon, mastic, red myrrh, mace, and cloves. Take a half-pound of the juice of honey and five pounds of rectified aqua vitae.

Let all these be digested together for nine days; afterward, let them be separated and distilled in a pelican <term>Pelican|A specialized alchemical glass vessel with two "arms" that feed condensed liquid back into the main body, allowing for continuous circulation and refinement of the substance.</term> to extract the spirit. Finally, a roasted apple, crushed, and the spices that follow should be added to this liquid. Take two drams each of cinnamon, cloves, and mace; a half-dram each of rosemary flowers <note>Original: anthos</note> and wallflower; two drams of ambergris; five grains of musk; a half-dram of civet <note>A pungent secretion from the civet cat, used then as a medicinal fixative and perfume.</note>; one and a half drams each of ginger, cubebs, and nutmeg; two drams of amomum; two and a half drams of zedoary <note>A root similar to ginger but with a more bitter, camphor-like flavor.</note>; and one and a half drams of grains of paradise. 

Let them all be digested over hot ashes for three days. After digestion, separate the liquid and store it. Give five grains each day.

**From the Book on Contractures, Treatise 2, Chapter 4.** <note>Contractures refer to the permanent tightening of muscles, tendons, or skin, often causing joints to become fixed in a rigid position.</note>

# BOOK ONE. 179

## Certain experimental and curative remedies for removing symptoms or any complications in wounds.

### First Experiment.

Take **Hepatic Aloe** <note>original: "aloe epaticum"; a variety of aloe resin named for its dark, liver-like color, used in wound care for its drying and healing properties</note>, and distill it with raw <term>Tereniabin</term> <note>original: "Tereniabin"; a term of Persian origin often referring to manna or a specific resinous turpentine used in medieval pharmacy</note> into an oil, which by the best right can be called a **Balsam**. For with it are cured gangrene and cavernous ulcers—those which lost and destructive physicians falsely call by the fictional name of "The Penance of St. John" or "The Vengeance," etc. <note>The author is criticizing contemporary doctors who gave religious or superstitious names to severe infections to hide their inability to treat them.</note>

### Second Experiment.

Take 3 ounces of **Sal Ammoniac** <note>original: "Salis armoniaci"; ammonium chloride</note>.
Take half an ounce of **common salt**.
Place them in water and let them boil. Afterward, add 3 ounces of **atramentary gum** <note>original: "gummi atramentarij"; a preparation likely containing vitriol (metal sulfates) and plant gum, used for its astringent and cleansing qualities</note>.
Let them boil again, and let them be cooked down until they reach a state of hardness. Throw a piece of this, about the size of a bean, into a measure of water; any ulcers washed with this water are cleansed and become as if fresh, most excellently disposed for healing.

### Third Experiment.

Let the three veins located at the feet be opened <note>A reference to phlebotomy or bloodletting, intended to divert "corrupt humors" away from the site of the ulcer</note>. Afterward, let **Hepatic Aloe** and raw **Tereniabin** be boiled together. Let the ulcers be washed with this decoction.

### Fourth Experiment.

Place **Parthenion** <note>original: "Parthenionem"; likely feverfew or pellitory-of-the-wall</note> and **Golden Consolida** <note>original: "consolidam auream"; usually refers to Goldenrod, a plant used to "consolidate" or knit together the edges of a wound</note>, crushed together, upon the ulcers; let them remain for three...

<summary>This page provides four specific medicinal recipes (experiments) for treating gangrene and persistent ulcers using resins, salts, and herbs. The author notably criticizes physicians who use superstitious labels for diseases they cannot cure.</summary>

Mix all these together. This medicine has bought a new set of clothes for many doctors. <note>An idiomatic way of saying the remedy was so successful and popular that it made the physicians wealthy.</note>

Mix together and smear over the ulcers.

This medicine has relieved countless most-learned <note>original: "decoctissimos"; literally "well-boiled," a pun meaning the doctors were either very experienced/seasoned or perhaps financially "cooked" before finding success with this cure.</note> doctors from their misery.
